  - **29 Jul 2026 — twenty-first run, and the first of LAP 2 (the confidence interval as a reported object, a self-contradiction between two of the site's own pages, and a checker for worked examples that do not hold together).** The ledger was empty, so this run took a thread rather than a staleness order: five pages that all promise an interval and stop short of one. `apa.html`, `cheat-apa.html`, `writing/reporting-statistics-apa` (§3.2), `stats-1/effect-size-and-power` (§1.13) and `stats-2/correlation` (§2.8). **The thread was chosen from the site's own words:** §3.2 tells readers that means, mean differences, correlations and regression coefficients "have no excuse at all" for omitting an interval, and the site's own APA formatter omitted them for all four. **Absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep excluding `.claude/worktrees` and the generated index.** (1) **`Fisher z` appeared exactly once on the whole site** — as a parenthetical label in `correlation.html`'s readout, "95% CI for *r* (Fisher *z*)" — with `r-to-z`, `Fisher's z` and `z transformation` all at zero and the transformation itself explained nowhere; that is `widget-terms.js`'s defect shape on a surface `widget-terms.js` does not scan. §2.8 gained **"The interval around *r*"**, worked on the lesson's own numbers: *r* = .42 at *n* = 60 gives *z* = .448, SE = 1/√57 = .132, interval [.188, .707] on the transformed scale and **95% CI [.19, .61]** back on the correlation scale, sitting .23 below the estimate and .19 above it. Halving that width to ±.10 takes **n = 263**. (2) **`90% CI` returned zero sitewide**, on a site that teaches η² in five lessons; §3.2 gained the paragraph, with the reason rather than the rule — the *F* test is one-tailed, so at 90% the lower limit leaves zero exactly when *p* reaches .05, while a 95% interval holds on to zero until *p* reaches .025 and a result significant at .05 but not at .025 therefore gets a 95% interval starting at exactly zero. For §3.2's own ANOVA the two are **90% CI [.02, .21] against 95% CI [.01, .23]**. (3) **`stats-1/effect-size-and-power` had zero occurrences of "confidence interval"** on a lesson that teaches Cohen's *d*; it gained **"How precisely do you know *d*?"**, worked on the playground's own dials: at the defaults (*d* = 0.50, 30/group) the test gives *t*(58) = 1.94, *p* = .058 and an interval of **[−0.02, 1.01]**; at the 64/group the same widget asks for at 80% power, *t*(126) = 2.83, *p* = .005 and **[0.15, 0.85]** — a study that worked and still cannot separate small from large. Pinning *d* to ±0.20 takes **199 per group**, three times what 80% power costs. (4) **`cheat-apa.html` mentioned confidence intervals nowhere**, while its own card ④ told readers to pair *p* with an effect size "ideally a CI"; it gained card **⑥ The interval, in brackets**, and still fits one page (A4 200.5 mm of 279 mm, US Letter 200.5 of 261, measured the P30 way by flipping every `@media print` rule's `mediaText` to include `screen`). **THE BUILD: `apa.html` now emits an interval for every pattern, which was the oldest item on the list at nine runs.** `viz.js` gained the effect-size CI block — `nctCI` / `varExpCI` / `vCI` / `rCI` plus the two solvers underneath — because for *d*, partial η², R² and Cramér's *V* there is no ± formula and the interval has to be found by inverting the test. **Every published number was reproduced independently in R 4.5.2** by inverting `pt`/`pf`/`pchisq(ncp=)` with `uniroot`, agreeing to **4 dp on all eight anchors**, and the intervals were then checked for **coverage by simulation at 5,000 reps: .949 / .900 / .902 against nominal .95 / .90 / .90**. `math-check.js` grew a seventh section and went **270 → 297 assertions**. **The R cross-check earned its keep immediately: the first draft clamped the *t*'s noncentrality at zero**, which is right for the λ of a χ² or *F* (a sum of squares) and wrong for a signed δ, and it was reporting *d* ≥ 0.00 for a *p* = .058 result whose honest answer is *d* ≥ −0.02. `deltaSolve` now brackets on both sides and math-check asserts the sign behavior in both directions, including that δ_L passes through exactly 0 at the critical *t*. The tool also gained a **`k` = min(rows, cols) − 1 field** (Cramér's *V* cannot be bounded without the table's shape) and a second consistency note beside the statcheck one, for η² and R² against *F*. **A SELF-CONTRADICTION BETWEEN TWO PAGES, found by trying to write one sentence.** §1.13's power playground reported **n ≈ 63 per group for 80% power at *d* = 0.5** while `power.html`, `plan.html`, `problems.html` and `math-check.js` all say **64** (the G*Power anchor): the widget's readouts were normal-approximation, undeclared. The three printed numbers are now exact noncentral-*t* (**48% power, ±0.52 critical *d̂*, ≈ 64**), the two drawn curves stay normal because that is what makes the picture readable, and the code comment now says which is which. The `n80` search starts from the normal estimate, which measurement showed lands 0–3 below the exact answer across the whole slider range, and is cached on (*d*, α, tails) so dragging the sample-size slider never recomputes it. Verified in-browser: both sliders round-trip to byte-identical readouts. **FAQ freshness: two swaps, and two lessons deliberately left alone.** Out: §2.8's "What sample size do I need for a reliable correlation?", whose "estimates only start stabilizing around n ≈ 150–250" was folklore the new section replaces with an exact number; and §3.2's "Do I italicize *p* and *t*?" (**ECHO 67%**, answered in full by the lesson's own h2, again by the poster's card ③ and again by the formatter, and closing on an unverifiable superlative, "the most frequently flagged APA error there is"). In: **"My two groups both show a correlation, but one is bigger. Can I test whether they really differ?"** — `compare two correlations`, `two correlations` and `dependent correlations` all returned zero — carrying *r* = .55 against *r* = .30 at *n* = 60 each, **z = 1.65, *p* = .099**, intervals [.34, .71] and [.05, .51], and **168 per group** to detect that gap at 80% power (all four numbers run in Node and again in R); and **"My output has no confidence interval on the effect size. Do I leave it out?"**, which is precise about SPSS being inconsistent with itself (one-way ANOVA has printed an *ANOVA Effect Sizes* table with a 95% interval since v27, GLM Univariate still prints partial η² with none) and ends on what not to do. §1.13's three were kept whole. Still **291 Q&As, 291 unique question strings, 3 per lesson**. **software.js: rotated to four untouched by runs 18–20** — `correlation`, `one-way-anova`, `simple-linear-regression`, `chi-square-tests` — against **SPSS 32.0.x** (still current, GA 23 Apr 2026) and **JASP 0.98.1** (7 Jul 2026, still current; 0.98.0 added JASP AI and 0.98.1 is a Repeated Measures ANOVA fix, no menu renames), both re-checked against the vendors' own release notes rather than from memory. Every statistic re-derived: correlation *r* = .42/df 58 → *t* = 3.53, *p* < .001 with CI [.19, .61] exact; one-way *F*(2, 87) = 5.42 → *p* = .0060, η² = .1108; regression *b*/*SE* = 4.053 → 4.05 with CI [1.16, 3.46] exact and R² = .25 matching *F* = 16.40 to .2547. **One genuine defect:** the chi-square entry reported "68% of treated patients recovered versus 45% of controls" beside χ²(1, *N* = 120) = 6.25, and **no integer 2×2 table with *N* = 120 produces both** (an exhaustive scan over every row split found none). It now reads 41 of 60 against 27 of 60, **χ² = 6.65, *p* = .010, φ = .24, 90% CI [.09, .39]**. The other three entries gained the intervals `apa.html` now emits. Still 34 entries. **Snippets: both subject entries extended in step**, with **every R line executed here on R 4.5.2 exactly as shipped** (the two-correlation Fisher-*z* test returns *p* = .0992; the `uniroot` interval on *d* returns [0.15, 0.85], and the bracket was narrowed to *t* ± 6 specifically to stop R emitting a `pnt{final}` precision warning a student would not know to ignore). The Python correlation snippet moved to the modern `stats.pearsonr(...).confidence_interval()` (SciPy 1.11+). **Link health, measured.** No teaching page sits at 0 inbound; the six zeroes are navigation and utility pages reached from the nav by design. Six natural placements raised run 20's two flagged targets: **`cheat-apa.html` 2 → 3, `correlation.html` 2 → 3, `apa.html` 3 → 5**. **Quips: five reviewed, three rewritten.** `reporting-statistics-apa`, `cheat-apa` and `apa` all opened "Italic *t*, upright η², …" — one template, three surfaces; two now open differently and the third lost a clause. **Backlog item — no Backlog section exists (P37 is still unrun), so as in runs 11–20 the item became a checker, and this run's own hand-finds defined it. `tools/worked-examples.js`: does a published worked example hold together?** `audit.js` CHECK 9 recomputes the *p* beside a statistic; nothing checked the **effect size** in the same sentence, and this run found four of those by hand — apa.html's R² default (.34 against an *F* implying .31), apa.html's one-sample *d* default (0.42 against a *t* implying 0.41), software.js's chi-square percentages, and cheat-apa.html's worked sentence (M/SD pairs producing *t* = 2.44, not the *t* = 2.02 printed two clauses later; its SDs are now 2.5 and 2.8, which reproduce *t* = 2.022, *p* = .0466 and *d* = 0.452 exactly). **Two of the four live in a tool's field defaults**, so the script renders `apa.html`'s nine default sentences under a DOM shim and checks those too. **Rounding is handled exactly rather than with a fudge factor**: a published *F* of 5.42 stands for [5.415, 5.425), the identity is monotone in *F*, and the flag fires only when the implied band fails to overlap the band the published effect size stands for — no tolerance constant appears in the file. **Three gates were arrived at by measuring.** Requiring no other statistic between the halves kills the one false pairing in the corpus (a greedy window hands `F(1, 76) = 9.31`'s η² to the `F(1, 76) = 0.31` three clauses earlier). **Reading only forward missed every R² on the site, including the defect the script was written for**, because `R² = .31, 90% CI […], F(3, 96) = 14.20` is the ordinary APA order — so it reads both directions. And both windows stop at the nearest **clause** boundary rather than a full stop, which is precisely what separates the legitimate backward pair from the false one (a colon). Plain η² is the one judgment call and ships with **one `ACKED` entry** (a repeated-measures example where η² is legitimately smaller than partial η²). **Its blind spots are in the header rather than smoothed over: two of the four defects that motivated it are outside its reach** — recovering an integer 2×2 table from two rounded percentages, or a *t* from two M/SD pairs without the group split, is a search rather than an identity. **Proved on four reintroduced defects across three surfaces** (both apa.html defaults, an η²p in lesson HTML, an η² in a software.js walkthrough): each fired with exit 1, each restore returned the file byte-identical and the exit code to 0. It reports **31 pairs** today. **A SEARCH DEFECT THE INDEX-SEARCH STEP CAUGHT, and it was not about this run's terms.** Searching the rebuilt index for everything added, "Fisher's transformation" came back empty in the live overlay while sitting plainly in the index. `flexRe` allowed **only a space or hyphen** between query characters while `squash()` strips **every** non-alphanumeric out of the query, so any apostrophe in the corpus blocked a match and **`Cohen's d` — about as ordinary a query as this site can receive — returned nothing at all**. Widening the class to any single non-alphanumeric took **9 of 15 natural possessive queries from zero hits to hits** (Cohen's d 0 → 15, Levene's test 0 → 7, Cook's distance 0 → 5, Tukey's HSD 0 → 3, McNemar's test 0 → 2) while `ab`/`xy`/`zz`/`qq`/`test`/`data`/`mean`/`sd` returned **identical** counts and the full-corpus scan stayed at 15 ms per 20 passes. It stays bounded at **one** separator: two would let `90% CI` match but would also let a query drift across unrelated words, so that case is noted for the next run rather than fixed. **Verification.** `audit.js` **0 errors / 20 warnings / 64 info** (baseline), `math-check.js` **297 assertions, 0 failures**, and `prose-lint`, `faq-audit`, `widget-terms`, `link-promises`, `advice-terms`, `untaught-names` and the new `worked-examples` all **exit 0 under `--strict`**. **Zero em-dashes were added to any page's prose** — per-page counts held at 9 / 8 / 6 / 5 and `cheat-apa.html` fell 4 → 3 — while the five subjects grew by **434 / 338 / 212 / 130 / 128 words**; every em-dash in an added line is pre-existing text swept up by a rewritten line, which the unchanged counts confirm. The diff read-back caught two things no linter sees: a signposting sentence opening the *d*-interval identity ("One identity is worth carrying out of this"), deleted outright, and a FAQ answer opening "Yes, and…", which is the verdict reflex VOICE rule 7 bans in its comma form. **137 pages · 534 inline scripts · 235 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures**; all shared JS, `sw.js`, the seven tools and `faq_data.py` parse.   - **28 Jul 2026 — twentieth run (the Stats 4 psychophysics pair, the folk claim that turned out to be conditional, and a checker for names the site drops and never picks up). LAP 1 IS COMPLETE: all 97 lessons have now been subjects.** The ledger named the last two and this run took them: `stats-4/psychometric-functions` (§4.11) and `stats-4/signal-detection-theory` (§4.12). They share a defect shape, which is what made them a genuine pair rather than a leftover: **each names the procedure that produced its data, or the assumption its arithmetic rests on, and stops before the machinery.** **Five absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep excluding the stale clones under `.claude/worktrees` and the generated `search-index.js`.** (1) **`staircase` appeared on the site three times and never as a psychophysical method** (the other two are a Kaplan–Meier step function and a regression tree), with `1-up`, `transformed up-down`, `Levitt`, `QUEST`, `method of limits`, `method of adjustment` and `adaptive method` all at zero — on a lesson whose own parenthetical says adaptive procedures "reach the threshold with fewer trials". §4.11 gained **"How adaptive procedures find the threshold"**, and **the folk claim did not survive being simulated, which is the most useful thing this run did.** On the lesson's own observer (PSE 500 ms, σ 80 ms, 280 trials, matching its 7 × 40 ladder), the staircase's *placement* is superb — **97% of its trials land within 80 ms of the true PSE against the ladder's one level in seven**, and by Fisher information the **160 trials at 200/300/700/800 ms carry 7%** of what the experiment learns while the 40 at 500 ms carry 44%. But the textbook estimator gives it all back: **averaging the last eight reversals recovers the PSE with SD 24 ms against the ladder's 11 ms, and stops improving after about forty trials**, because eight reversals are eight numbers however long you run. **Fit the psychometric function to all 280 staircase trials and the SD drops to 6 ms, so the fixed ladder needs about 790 trials, 2.8×, to match it.** The honest moral, which is now the section's ending, is that adaptivity buys placement and freedom from having to know the answer in advance, not a free pass on the analysis: with a true PSE of 525 ms and a sharp σ of 25 ms, only one rung of the ladder falls on the rising part of the curve and **constant stimuli returns 505 ms, out by twenty, while the staircase lands on 525.0**. Levitt's transformed rules are stated with their convergence points (`p^k = ½` → **70.7%** and **79.4%**) and confirmed by simulation at **.705** and **.790**. Everything here was run twice, in Node against `VIZ` and independently in **R 4.5.2** (R gave SD 10.5 / 22.9 / 6.2 and 800 trials to match, against Node's 10.6 / 23.7 / 6.3 and 791). (2) **`2AFC` and `chance level` returned zero, `forced choice` zero in this sense, and `guess rate` appeared once — inside §4.11's own FAQ answer, undefined** — while §4.12's FAQ leans on "a two-alternative comparison" as its anchor for what a *d′* means. §4.11 gained **"When the curve cannot start at zero"**: the ψ(x) = γ + (1 − γ − λ)F(x) form, the 75% threshold convention, and the damage measured rather than asserted. Fitting a plain two-parameter curve to 2AFC data from the lesson's own observer puts the threshold at **280 ms against a true 500** and inflates the JND from 54 ms to 160 ms; holding γ at .5 recovers it to within a millisecond. The lapse case is gentler and aimed at the number people report: a **4% lapse rate leaves the PSE at 500 ms and takes the JND to 73 ms against a true 54, a third too wide** (R independently: 73.2 ms, +36%), returning to 53 ms once the lapse is in the model — which puts a magnitude on a claim §4.11's own FAQ has always made without one. The section also lands the identity nothing on the site stated, **Pc = Φ(d′/√2), the same formula as the AUC identity**, so 76% correct is *d′* = 1 (.7602) and 92% is *d′* = 2 (.9214) — the two anchors §4.12's FAQ was already quoting. (3) **`Weber's law` appeared exactly once sitewide**, in a subordinate clause of §4.11 ("the lifted-weight experiments that gave us Weber's law"), with the law never stated; `Fechner` once, `Stevens' power law` and `absolute threshold` zero. It now gets a paragraph, and it earns its place by explaining a fact the lesson already asserted and never justified: **if equal ratios feel equally different, the bisection point sits at the geometric mean**, √(200 × 800) = 400 ms, because 400 is double 200 and 800 is double 400. (4) **`optimal criterion` returned zero and `prior odds`/`posterior odds` appeared only in `problems.html`**, on a lesson that says criterion placement "is where costs, rewards, and base rates enter the decision" and gives no rule. §4.12 gained **"Where the criterion should sit"** with β_opt = [P(N)/P(S)] × [cost of a false alarm / cost of a miss] and *c*_opt = ln(β_opt)/*d′*, **every case confirmed by a numeric scan of expected value over λ to four decimals**: at the playground's own *d′* = 1.5, equal base rates and equal costs give *c* = 0 (the slider's home position), a 10% base rate gives β = 9 and *c* = **+1.465** (hits 23.7%, false alarms 1.3%), and a smoke alarm whose miss costs twenty times a false alarm gives β = .05 and *c* = **−2.00**. The case worth teaching is the one where both forces pull: **5 in 1,000 prevalence against a miss costing a hundred times a recall gives β = 199/100 = 1.99 and *c* = +0.459**, barely conservative, hit rate 61.4%, false alarms 11.3% — and the live widget at its nearest step (*c* = 0.45) reads **61.8% / 11.5%**, so the section is checkable on its own page. (5) **`zROC` returned zero and every one of the 14 "unequal variance" hits was Welch or homoscedasticity**, on a lesson that states "for the equal-variance model, AUC = Φ(*d′*/√2)" and never says how you would know. §4.12 gained **"When the two bells are not the same width"**, and the table it now carries is the reason the section exists: with the signal distribution at 1.5 with SD 1.25 against standard-normal noise (zROC slope **.800**, intercept **1.200**, both exact), **plain *d′* walks from 1.10 to 1.60 across six criterion placements** — one observer, one true sensitivity, six answers, and the only thing that changed is willingness to say yes. **d_a = 1.325 and A_z = .8256**, verified three independent ways: closed form, numeric integration under the ROC (.825672), and a **forty-million-draw Monte Carlo in R (.825570)**. Equal-variance readings of that same curve would have claimed areas from .782 to .871. **FAQ freshness: two swaps, one deliberate keep, scored with `faq-audit.js` and then judged by hand.** Out: §4.11's "What is the difference between the PSE and the JND?" (**ECHO 67%** against its own h2 "Two numbers: PSE and JND", which answers it in full) and §4.12's "What is the difference between *d′* and the criterion *c*?" (**ECHO 50%** against "Sweep the criterion…", **TWIN question 50%** with `reporting-statistics-apa` #2, and answered outright by the lesson's own "Skill or strategy" section). Both were "what is the difference between X and Y" openers, so the census the ledger tracks drops by two. In: **"Can I pool everyone's trials into one curve?"** — `pooling across`, `aggregate the data` and `pooled curve` all returned zero, and the answer carries an exact identity plus a simulation (a mixture of shifted cumulative Gaussians has spread **√(σ² + τ²)**; 24 observers with a true JND of 54 ms whose PSEs scatter with SD 60 ms give a pooled curve reading **67 ms, a quarter too wide**, closed form 67.45, while the PSE comes back correct) — and **"My task was two-alternative forced choice, not yes/no. Does *d′* still work?"**, which is where the Pc = Φ(*d′*/√2) conversion belongs. **§4.12's "What is a good *d′* value?" was kept despite the highest twin on the page** (question **67%** with `roc-curves-and-auc` #1) because only the template twins: the two answers share nothing in substance and this is the exact query students type. Still **291 Q&As, 291 unique question strings, 3 per lesson**. **software.js: rotated to four untouched by runs 17–19** — `psychometric-functions`, `ancova`, `factor-analysis-pca` and `survival-analysis` — against **SPSS 32.0.x (GA 23 Apr 2026, still current; its headline addition is a native *Analyze → Mediation Analysis*, which a previous run had already caught in the mediation entry)** and **JASP 0.98.1 (7 Jul 2026, still current; 0.98.1 is a Repeated Measures ANOVA fix plus help-file translations, no menu renames)**. Every statistic re-derived: ancova **F(1, 57) = 6.84 → p = .0114 → .011**, ηp² = 6.84/(6.84 + 57) = **.107 → .11**, slopes check **F(1, 56) = 0.42 → p = .5196 → .52**, and the df pair 57/56 is exactly right for N = 60 with one factor and one covariate; factor analysis **χ²(190) = 1438.2 → p ≈ 1.8e-190**, and df 190 implies **20 items**, consistent with the 9 + 7 that load; survival **χ²(1) = 8.42 → p = .00371 → .004** with the **HR CI log-symmetric** (ln 1.83 = .6043 against a midpoint of .6047) implying z = 2.86, p = .0042, coherent with the log-rank; psychometric **z = 3.46 → p = 5.4e-4**, CI [−72, −20] centred exactly on Δ = −46 with an implied SE of 13.30. **One genuine drift fix:** the JASP steps for `survival-analysis` stopped at Kaplan–Meier while the tips told readers to report a hazard ratio, leaving a JASP reader with no way to get one — the module has offered **semi-parametric Cox regression since 0.19.2 and parametric survival since 0.95**, and the steps now say so. Still 34 entries. **Snippets: both subject entries extended in step with the new sections** — a fixed-guess-rate ML fit for 2AFC and the zROC → *d_a* → *A_z* chain, in both languages — with **every R line executed here on R 4.5.2 exactly as shipped** (the zROC block run on the rounded rates printed in §4.12's own table returns slope **.801**, intercept **1.199**, *d_a* **1.324**, *A_z* **.825**, which is an independent check that the published table is internally consistent). A scan of all 77 snippet entries for deprecated APIs (`aes_string`, `groupby().apply`, `np.float`, `distplot`, `gather`/`spread`, `funs()`, `sklearn.cross_validation`, …) found **nothing** beyond the pandas fix run 18 made; the one hit was `list.append` in a toy Markov snippet, a false positive. **Link health, measured.** No teaching page sits at 0 inbound; the four zeroes are navigation and utility pages reached from the nav by design, as in runs 18–19. **No lesson sits at 0 or 1 outbound** (the minimum is 2), so both of step 7's questions are answered and the next weakest target is inbound: `tables.html` was at **4** and gains a genuinely natural placement in §4.12, whose worked example does two z-transforms by hand. **`cheat-apa.html` and `correlation.html` sit at 2 each and were deliberately left**, since forcing either into a psychophysics lesson is the over-linking step 7 warns against; they are flagged in the ledger for the run that takes a lesson where they fit. **Quips: five reviewed, three kept, two shortened**, both for the same defect — a closing clause that only labels the joke it follows. §4.11's lost "That's the PSE." and `variables-and-operationalization` lost "That's operationalization.", which is the shorter fix VOICE.md asks for. **Backlog item — no Backlog section exists (P37 is still unrun), so as in runs 11–19 the item became a checker, and this run's own first find defined it. `tools/untaught-names.js`: does the site NAME a method and never say what it is?** The three existing term checkers read what an *interactive prints*, what *advice strings say* and what a *link anchor promises*; ordinary lesson prose was the surface none of them touched, and `Weber's law` sat in it, used once, explained nowhere. **The rule:** an eponym (capitalized surname + possessive + a head noun from a closed list that makes it a method) occurring **exactly once sitewide** is a name the site drops and never picks up. **Five definitions were measured against the corpus and discarded first.** "Never wrapped in `<strong>`" as the proxy for untaught gave **10** flags including `Levene's test` and `Cohen's kappa`, which the site plainly teaches — bolding is a first-use convention, not a completeness guarantee. Any possessive plus any lowercase word gave **69** flags of "That's the", "Let's build", "Marek's neighbor". Not normalizing apostrophes inflated the once-only list **from 2 to 8**, because lesson HTML writes `Welch's` straight and `software.js` writes it curly, so half that list was one term counted as two. **An acronym class was built, measured and dropped:** after filtering ordinary words set in capitals (`PARTICIPATE`, `CONSENT`, `OMNIBUS`) and indexed series (`PC3` in "variance in PC1-PC3"), **26** flags remained and they were `BBC`, `USB`, `USD`, `API`, `OS`, `UX`, `WCAG`, `WWII` beside `MCMC`, `JAGS` and `QRP` — what makes an acronym owed an explanation is its *domain*, which cannot be read off its shape, and the alternative is the hand-kept inventory VOICE.md rule 12 documents as the mistake. **And the first shout filter lower-cased the text before collecting its vocabulary, so every acronym inserted its own lowercase form and masked itself** — GDPR, MCMC and SDT were all silently filtered and only acronyms containing a digit ever fired. **That bug was caught by the reintroduce-the-defect step and by nothing else, which is exactly why the step exists.** Development also walked straight into the glossary trap CLAUDE.md documents for `build-search-index.py`: `glossary-data.js` is plain text whose definitions contain `p < .05`, and running it through the HTML tag-strip ate most of the deck and made `Simpson's paradox` look like a once-only name when the glossary defines it perfectly well. **On its first clean run it found one defect on its own:** `software.js` called the MANOVA statistic **Hotelling's trace** while `stats-3/manova` only ever wrote **Hotelling–Lawley trace**, so a reader following the SPSS steps met a name the lesson never used and could not search for; both files now bridge the two names. It ships with an **empty `ACKED`** table. **Proved on two independent defect shapes:** deleting the paragraph that states Weber's law fired `Weber's law` and exit 1, and writing a fresh unexplained eponym (`Levitt's rule`) fired that and exit 1, both returning to exit 0 on restore with the file byte-identical to its pre-test state. Its blind spots are in the header rather than smoothed over: a name made of **ordinary lowercase words is invisible** to it, which is precisely how `staircase`, `guess rate` and the equal-variance assumption had to be found this run, by reading. **A REGRESSION THIS RUN CAUSED AND THE INDEX SEARCH CAUGHT.** The two subject lessons grew to **16,281 and 14,538 characters**, past `LESSON_MAX_CHARS`, and because the "Common questions" block sits at the END of the article both were truncated **exactly on their FAQ answers** — the identical defect run 13 found and fixed by raising the cap from 4,500 to 12,000, back after seven runs of 300-to-600-word additions. Searching the rebuilt index for every term this run added is what surfaced it (**4 MISS on the first pass**, two of them the new FAQ answers). **`LESSON_MAX_CHARS` raised 12,000 → 20,000**, which indexes every lesson whole (longest 16.3k) for **0.8% more index text**; the cap is a runaway guard, not a budget, and both the script and CLAUDE.md now say to check it first when a term cannot be found. Rebuilt index **919 KB, 97 lessons, 37 pages, 0 lessons truncated, 0 missing their FAQ block**, and the term search then returned **35 HIT, 2 MISS of 37** — the two remaining misses are `Semi-parametric` and `Cox regression` as written in a `software.js` walkthrough, and **`software.js` has never been a search-index source at all**; `Cox`, `proportional hazards` and `hazard ratio` all resolve to `survival-analysis`, so nothing is unreachable, but that whole injected surface is unindexed and is noted in the ledger. **Verification:** `audit.js` **0 errors / 20 warnings / 64 info** (baseline restored after the rebuild), `math-check.js` **270 assertions, 0 failures**, and `prose-lint`, `faq-audit`, `widget-terms`, `link-promises`, `advice-terms` and the new `untaught-names` all **exit 0 under `--strict`** — with **zero em-dashes added to any subject** (§4.11 stayed at 6 and its FAQ went 1 → 0; §4.12 stayed at 10 and its FAQ went 3 → 2) while those two pages grew by **1,048 and 747 words**. The diff read-back found 5 em-dashes in added lines and every one is pre-existing text swept up by a rewritten line or a regenerated FAQ block, which the unchanged per-page counts confirm; it also caught one thing the linter cannot see, a two-sentence build to a reveal opening the criterion section, and the shortest fix flattened it into one plain sentence. **3 changed pages · 12 inline scripts · 6 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures**; all shared JS, `sw.js`, the seven tools and `faq_data.py` parse. Browser pass over all three changed pages via same-origin iframes at **1200px, 375px and 360px**: every expected string present, **0 console errors, 0 leading-slash links, 0 horizontal scroll at any width**, every lesson `h2` carrying an id, all 8 new or edited link targets returning **200**, the new `.ref-table` auto-wrapped in `.hscroll`, and §4.11's sliders round-tripping to the exact same six readouts (frozen-noise law intact). Dark mode verified with `localStorage.theme` set before load: bg `#0b1120`, `--link` `#6f7bf2`, canvases painted, and the new table measured at **8.91:1** body and **7.34:1** header contrast with the new prose link at **5.19:1** against the surface and **3.06:1** against body text, clearing both gates `--link` is tuned for. **`CACHE_VERSION` bumped sc-v38 → sc-v39** (two QUIPS changed and `site.js` is precached; `snippets.js` and `software.js` also changed but are lazy-loaded and never precached). **Three notes for the next run, which is the first of lap 2.** The ledger is empty and lap 1 is over, so read the lap-2 note under the P39 prompt before choosing anything. **`apa.html` still emits no confidence interval** — eight runs old, and now the only item on that list. And a standing observation this run chose not to act on: run 19 kept §4.1's Tuskegee FAQ at REPRISE 42% for the same reason this run kept §4.12's *d′* question, that a high score on a question students actually type is not a reason to swap it; two runs have now made that call, and it is probably time to say so in `faq-audit.js`'s header rather than rediscovering it a third time.
  - **28 Jul 2026 — nineteenth run (the inferential-foundations arc, five lessons that name an assumption and never say how you would know, and a checker for the terms the site's own advice uses without teaching).** The ledger named the thread and this run took it: `stats-1/sampling-distributions` (§1.7), `stats-1/central-limit-theorem` (§1.8), `stats-2/non-parametric-alternatives` (§2.6), `stats-3/assumptions-of-regression` (§3.8) and `stats-4/generalized-linear-models` (§4.4), which read as one argument about what a model assumes of its data. **95 of 97 lessons have now been subjects, and every course except the Stats 4 psychophysics pair is finished.** They share a defect shape, which is what made them a coherent five: each states a condition and stops before the number that would tell you whether the condition holds. **Five absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep that excluded the stale clones under `.claude/worktrees` and the generated `search-index.js`.** (1) **`relative efficiency`, `efficient estimator`, `efficiency of the`, `standard error of the median`, `consistent estimator` and `biased estimator` all returned zero**, on a lesson whose widget offers *Sample median* as a collectible statistic, prints its standard error, and says only that the distribution "changes shape and width". §1.7 gained **"Which estimate should you trust?"**, naming unbiasedness, consistency and efficiency, with the trade measured on the widget's **own three populations** (400,000 draws each at *n* = 25): uniform **SE 5.77 vs 9.61**, bimodal **4.68 vs 13.45**, skewed **3.16 vs 3.23**. Two of those three rows are teaching points in their own right and both were verified against closed form (uniform σ/√n = **5.774**, bimodal **4.682**). The bimodal row is the extreme case because almost nobody in that population sits near 50, so **74% of sample medians land outside the 40-to-60 band**; the skewed row is where efficiency is the wrong question outright, since that population's mean is **15.96** and its median **16·ln2 = 11.09**, so the two statistics estimate different numbers. The normal-population anchor is exact: **√(π/2) = 1.2533**, so a median costs **π/2 ≈ 1.57×** the participants (simulation gave 1.2434 at n = 25, 1.2356 at n = 50). (2) **`finite variance` and `infinite variance` returned zero, and `Cauchy` appeared once, as a Bayes prior scale in `software.js`** — on a lesson whose lede says "almost any distribution" and states no condition at all. §1.8 gained **"The fine print on 'almost any'"**: the Cauchy counterexample, measured (400,000 repeats, the middle half of the averages spans **2.00 units at n = 1 and 2.02 at n = 1,000**, against the exact Cauchy IQR of 2), and then the question the folk rule dodges. On the playground's **own** Skewed population (exponential, mean 15.96, skewness **1.8403**), an ordinary 95% *t* interval covers **90.2 / 92.8 / 93.6 / 94.3 / 94.7%** at n = **10 / 30 / 50 / 100 / 200** over 300,000 simulated studies each, and the failures are lopsided: at n = 30 the interval lands entirely **below** μ 6.4% of the time against **0.8%** above. The mechanism is stated and checked rather than asserted — the sampling distribution of the mean carries the population's skewness divided by √n, measured **0.336** at n = 30 against a predicted 1.8403/√30 = **0.336** — so leftover skew fades on the same schedule the standard error does. A named rule of thumb (Cochran's n > 25γ₁²) was drafted and **cut** because its published guarantee could not be reproduced against these coverage numbers; the measured table ships instead. (3) **`rank-biserial` appeared in exactly ONE file sitewide, `software.js`** — where the JASP walkthrough tells readers to tick *Effect size* and the tip offers "*r* = *z*/√*N* (or the rank-biserial correlation from JASP)" as if the two were interchangeable. Nothing on the site defined either. §2.6 gained **"The effect size your software prints"**, anchored to the ledger widget's own default tab, whose readouts were then read back off the live page to confirm them: rank sums **23 / 55**, ***U*** **= 2**, ***z*** **= 2.48**, ***p*** **= .013**, **P(superiority) = .94**. So *r*<sub>rb</sub> = 2 × .944 − 1 = **.8889 → .89**, independently reproduced in R from `wilcox.test()`'s own *W* (**1 − 2U/n₁n₂ = 0.8889**), against ***r*** = *z*/√*N* = 2.4820/√12 = **.7165 → .72** — two different numbers for one dataset, which is exactly why the tip needed fixing. (4) **`hat matrix`, `studentized residual`, `DFBETA`, `DFFITS`, `White test`, `Huber` and `2p/n` all returned zero**, on the lesson whose widget prints a leverage readout. The find underneath was better than the absence: the widget graded the point "low / moderate / high" at hardcoded cutoffs of **0.15 and 0.28**, which are exactly **p/n = 2/14 = 0.1429** and **2p/n = 0.2857** for its 13 fixed points plus the dragged one — the textbook rule, encoded in the UI and stated nowhere — **and the "high" branch could never fire**, because the dragged point is clamped to x ∈ [5, 95] and tops out at **h = 0.2737**. The readout now prints the hat value and its multiple of average leverage (verified live: **0.07 (0.5× average)** parked at center, **0.26 (1.8×)** at the wall, and the arrow-key round trip returns exactly), and §3.8 gained **"How high is 'high'?"**: leverages sum to *p* (checked, **2.000000** across all 14 points), average leverage is p/n, the flags are 2p/n and 3p/n, and Cook's distance in closed form **D = (r²/p) × h/(1 − h)** is the lesson's own sentence "influence requires both" written as arithmetic. **One correction to the run's own first draft:** SPSS's *Casewise diagnostics* box flags on the plain **standardized** residual, not the studentized one, which is a separate tick under *Save…*; the prose and the new glossary entry both say so. (5) **`offset`, `log exposure`, `person-time` and `incidence rate` returned zero in the modeling sense** (all 17 `offset` hits were `offsetWidth`-style CSS/JS or the word in ordinary prose), on a lesson whose lede promises "counts, yes/no outcomes, and **rates**" and whose own bullet names a "multiplicative rate ratio". §4.4 gained **"Counts are not rates"**, worked on two clinics and **fitted in R rather than asserted**: 40 falls over 500 patient-days against 60 over 1,500 gives **exp(b₁) = 0.500 with `offset = log(days)`** and **1.500 without it** — opposite findings from one dataset. The section then closes the loop the lesson's own FAQ opens by referring to "deviance far exceeding its degrees of freedom" while the body never defines deviance: the deviance/df ratio is named, tied to the site's own practice problem (**412.6 / 178 = 2.318 → 2.32**), and calibrated on the widget's exact generator (N = 32, b₀ = 0.2, b₁ = 0.28, x ~ U(0,10)) by refitting it 20,000 times in R — **mean 1.0712, with 90% of fits between 0.67 and 1.55** — so the honest warning about how noisy the ratio is at that size ships with the rule. **Glossary: ten terms added (252 → 262), which was itself a finding.** The site teaches logistic regression through a link function with no glossary entry for *Link function*; §3.8 is entirely about leverage with no entry for *Leverage*; *Cook's distance* sits in six files with none. Added: Cook's distance, Deviance, Efficiency (of an estimator), Leverage (hat value h), Link function, Offset (in a GLM), Overdispersion, Rank-biserial correlation, Studentized residual, Unbiased estimator — each pointing at a ready lesson (verified: **0 entries point at a non-ready slug**), and each therefore also a new flashcard. **FAQ freshness: four swaps and one lesson deliberately left alone, scored with `faq-audit.js` and then judged by hand.** Out: §1.7's "Does every statistic have a sampling distribution?" (ECHO 67%, and a paraphrase of the lesson's own "Different statistics, different distributions" section — run 15's documented blind spot, where restating a section in fresh words scores REPRISE 0%); §1.8's "Why does the standard error shrink with the square root of n?" (REPRISE 6% and a near-verbatim restatement of the body's own "to halve the standard error you need four times the data", plus TWIN 29% with `sampling-distributions` #1); §3.8's "What is a high Cook's distance…?" (ECHO 33% against the new section that now carries the cutoffs, and answer TWIN **26%** with `regression-diagnostics` #3); and §4.4's "Which GLM family should I use for my outcome?" (ECHO 33%, a list restating the lesson's own "The family you already know"). In: "My estimator is unbiased. Doesn't that make it the right one to use?", "My data has a few enormous outliers. Will the CLT still rescue me?", **"Is there a real test for equal variances, or do I just eyeball the residual plot?"** — which fills a confirmed zero, since **`Breusch` appeared nowhere in prose and only inside `snippets.js` code** (`car::ncvTest()`, `het_breuschpagan()`), so the site ran a formal heteroscedasticity test in a snippet and never named it — and "My people were followed for different lengths of time. Can I just divide and model the rate?". **`stats-2/non-parametric-alternatives` was left at three untouched**: none restates an h2 or duplicates another page, and its Likert and "does Mann-Whitney compare medians" answers are the page's strongest search queries. Still **291 Q&As**, no duplicate question strings, and the opener census moved the right way (**"my-…" now 22, "what-is" down two**). **software.js: rotated to four untouched by runs 16–18** — `non-parametric-alternatives`, `regression-diagnostics`, `assumptions-and-when-they-break` and `logistic-regression` — against **SPSS 32.0.0 (GA 23 Apr 2026, still current)** and **JASP 0.98.1 (7 Jul 2026, still current)**, both re-checked against release notes this run rather than recalled. Every published statistic re-derived: Mann–Whitney **z = −2.13 → p = .0332 → .033**, Kruskal–Wallis **H(2) = 7.61 → p = .0223 → .022**, Levene **F(1, 58) = 8.42 → p = .0052 → .005**, logistic **Wald = (0.85/0.21)² = 16.38** against the printed 16.40 with **OR = e^0.85 = 2.3396 → 2.34** and **CI [1.5502, 3.5311] → [1.55, 3.53]** exact, and the model **χ²(2) = 28.7 → p = 5.9e-7**. The Mann–Whitney sentence's *U*, *z* and *r* were also checked for **mutual** consistency, which nothing on the site does: a grid search over n₁, n₂ shows **U = 245.5 with n₁ = 16, n₂ = 48 gives z = −2.147 and r = .268 → .27**, so a real design sits behind all three printed numbers. The SPSS claim that the Linear Regression dialog still has **no** heteroscedasticity-consistent standard-error option was re-verified against a current source (a 2026 *AMPPS* tutorial devoted to the workarounds) rather than memory. **No drift, so one fix rather than a rewrite:** the non-parametric tip offered *r* = *z*/√*N* "or the rank-biserial correlation" as alternatives, which §2.6 now shows are different numbers; it says so. The lesson's own city was then measured rather than described: **simulating the widget's exact algorithm** (400 populations × 400 draws) gives SRS SD **.0501** against cluster SD **.2467**, a **design effect of 24.3** (closed form 23.84, ρ = **.2370**), so its 80 clustered answers carry the information of about **3** independent ones. `quota sampling` was a smaller find of the same kind — it appears on the site **only as a distractor in a quiz question**, defined nowhere — and now gets a sentence naming why it looks like stratification and is not. (2) **`alpha spending`, `Pocock` and `O'Brien` returned zero hits, and `sequential analysis` appeared only inside a `software.js` JASP step**, on the lesson whose own fix prescribes "planned interim analyses with corrected thresholds rather than ad-hoc peeking" and names not one of them. §4.5 gained **"Peeking, done properly"**, calibrated on the lesson's **own** Monte-Carlo engine (one-sample two-tailed *t* via `VIZ.tUpper`, 300,000 trials). Five looks at n = 20…100: honest **.0499**, naive peeking **.1446**, a **Pocock constant of .0154** and an **O'Brien–Fleming sequence of .0000049 · .0012 · .0083 · .0223 · .0409** — which reproduce the published group-sequential tables (**.0158** and **.000005 · .0013 · .0084 · .0225 · .0413**) to the third decimal, an independent confirmation that the simulation is right. The trade-off is the reason to bother and is stated as one: at a true *d* = 0.4, a single final test at n = 100 has power **.978**, O'Brien–Fleming **.975 stopping at an average of 65 participants**, Pocock **.951 at an average of 52**. The widget's own 10-look default gives naive **.1980** and a Pocock constant of **.0102**. The section also reconciles this lesson with the site's own JASP walkthrough for `bayesian-estimation`, which tells readers to tick *Sequential analysis* and watch evidence accumulate; no page had ever squared those two. (5) **`45 CFR` returned zero and all six `Common Rule` hits were "common rule of thumb"**; `nonmaleficence`, `APA Ethics Code`, `code of conduct` and `professional code` were also zero. §4.1 gained **"From principles to the rules you actually work under"**: the Common Rule as 45 CFR 46 Subpart A, adopted in **1991 by fifteen federal departments and agencies at once**, Subparts B/C/D, the **2018 revision effective 21 January 2019**, the fact that *IRB* is an American word with national equivalents, and the professional-code layer — the APA's five General Principles (2002, amended 2010 and 2017), where Belmont's beneficence splits into **Beneficence and Nonmaleficence** and two principles appear that Belmont has no counterpart for, one of them **integrity**, which is why QRPs and authorship disputes are ethics violations with no harmed participant anywhere.
