  - **28 Jul 2026 — seventeenth run (the ML geometry spine, the counter the site showed and never named, and three rejected checkers before the one that worked).** The ledger named the thread and this run took it: the four never-opened ML lessons read as one arc about distance and shape — `knn-and-distance` (§5.8), `clustering-kmeans` (§5.9), `dimensionality-reduction` (§5.10), `neural-networks-intuition` (§5.11) — plus `data/data-privacy-basics` (§2.10) as the fifth, which is not a stretch: the classic re-identification results are a nearest-neighbour attack on high-dimensional sparse data, and the lesson's own demo shows uniqueness rising with every column kept. **85 of 97 lessons have now been subjects, and the ML course is finished.** **Five absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep that excluded the five stale clones under `.claude/worktrees` (they make a term look present when only a dead copy has it — worth remembering, since step 2's greps are this loop's most valuable instrument).** (1) **`Manhattan distance`, `cosine similarity`, `Minkowski` and `Gower` all returned zero hits**, on the lesson titled "k-NN & Why Distance Gets Weird" whose closing paragraph tells readers to reach for "a distance metric built for your data's structure" and then names none. §5.8 gained **"Which distance?"**, worked on a pair chosen so the two rulers disagree completely: against a profile of (5, 4, 5, 4, 5), a same-shape case at a fifth of the intensity sits at Euclidean **8.28** while a different-shape case at similar intensity sits at **5.66**, so Euclidean calls the same-shape case the far one — and cosine reverses it exactly, **1.000 against 0.843**. The section also carries a measured table of distance concentration (median over 400 reps, 500 uniform points): the farthest point is **47×** the nearest at *d* = 2, **3.4×** at 10, **1.16×** at 500, with Manhattan holding marginally more contrast at every dimension (**1.20 against 1.16** at 500), which is why L1 is recommended for wide data and why the section ends by calling it a tie-breaker rather than a rescue. (2) **`hierarchical clustering`, `dendrogram` and `linkage` returned zero hits sitewide**, on the lesson whose entire pain point is that you must name *k* before it will run. §5.9 gained the method that does not, and the number that matters was **run rather than asserted**: on two interlocking moons of 100 points each, **single linkage recovers both moons perfectly (100%) where k-means with 25 restarts manages 73%** — but complete, average and Ward all land near **82%**, so the honest moral is not "hierarchical instead of k-means" but that a chaining linkage can follow a curve and centroid methods cannot, with single linkage's own weakness (one stray point bridges two groups) named in the same breath. (3) **`component scores` and `principal component regression` returned zero hits**, and `stats-3/multicollinearity-and-variable-selection` has a "What to do about it" section containing **zero mentions of PCA** while the site teaches PCA in two separate lessons. §5.10 gained **"Putting the components to work"**, verified in R: four predictors, three of them near-copies, carry **VIFs near 7** raw and **exactly 1.000** as components (largest surviving correlation between scores **1.4e-15**), and the blind spot is quantified rather than hand-waved — **PC1 holds 70.7% of predictor variance and correlates .54 with the outcome while PC2 holds 24.4% and correlates .47**, and keeping all four reproduces the raw model's *R*² to the last digit (**.5238** both ways), so nothing is gained until you drop components and dropping is where a component that mattered for *y* goes out with the trash. (4) **`ReLU`, `rectified linear`, `softmax`, `vanishing gradient`, `mini-batch` and `stochastic gradient` all returned zero hits — and `epoch` appeared in exactly one file, `neural-networks-intuition` itself, only inside its own widget's status line.** The lesson shows the reader a live counter reading "Epoch 412 · loss 0.031" and never once says what an epoch is. §5.11 gained **"The words you meet in real code"**, and the run also **corrected the lesson's own definition**: it called an activation "a squashing function that maps it to a bounded output", which is false for ReLU, the default everywhere. ReLU's win is quantified — the sigmoid's slope peaks at exactly **0.25**, so ten layers shrink the gradient by at most **0.25¹⁰ ≈ 9.5e-7** while ReLU's positive-side slope is exactly 1 — and the epoch paragraph is anchored to the widget the reader is looking at (**56 training points, one update per pass, full-batch**, verified by reading `trainStep()`, which also revealed the hidden layer is **tanh**, a third activation the prose never named). (5) **`differential privacy`, `l-diversity` and `homogeneity attack` returned zero hits**, on a lesson that displays a sensitive diagnosis column beside a *k*-anonymity readout and tells readers higher *k* is safer. §2.10 gained **"What k-anonymity does not protect"**, and the numbers come from the lesson's **own shipped 30-row table**: generalizing both age and ZIP gives 15 groups of two, so *k* = 2 and every row turns green, yet **an attacker who pins you to your pair names your diagnosis 50% of the time against a 1-in-6 base rate** (the most common diagnosis is 5 of 30); matching on sex and ZIP region alone lifts *k* to 4 and the odds only fall to **36.7%**. Identity is bounded, the sensitive attribute is not. Differential privacy is named as the modern answer, with the 2020 US Census (TopDown) as its landmark deployment. **Three shipped defects on the privacy lesson, one of them a wrong human name.** The body called her **"Latham Sweeney"**; she is **Latanya Sweeney**, spelled correctly in the lesson's own FAQ two screens below. Worse, the site stated **"roughly 87% of Americans"** as unqualified fact in three places, and that figure is from the **1990** census: **Philippe Golle's 2006 re-analysis on 2000 census data puts it at 63%** (confirmed against two independent sources, not memory). All three now carry the lower number with the census year attached, including `ethics/privacy-and-confidentiality`'s FAQ, which is not a subject this run but shares the claim. And the lesson still promised that "a whole course of this toolkit is being built around" research ethics — **the Ethics course has been complete since P13**; it now links to §4.4. **FAQ freshness: three swaps and one extension, on merit rather than quota, scored with `faq-audit.js` and then judged by hand.** Out: §5.8's "What is the curse of dimensionality?" (**ECHO 100%** — it restated its own h2 verbatim), and two on §2.10 — "Is removing names enough to anonymize data?" (**TWIN 38% question / 22% answer** with `ethics/privacy-and-confidentiality` #2, "Is deleting participants' names enough to anonymize a dataset?", which is the same question on two pages) and "What is k-anonymity?" (**ECHO 100%** against its own h2, REPRISE 20%). In: "My training set has 200,000 rows. Will k-NN still work?" (**`KD-tree` and `ball tree` had zero hits**; the answer names the ~20-feature ceiling past which a spatial index stops beating brute force), "Should I be using differential privacy?" (honest answer: for a thesis, no) and "My dataset is k-anonymous. Is it safe to share?". `clustering-kmeans` #2 was **extended rather than swapped** — it recommended DBSCAN and spectral for curved shapes and, after this run, was the only place on the lesson that did not know single linkage also works. **§5.10 and §5.11 were left at three untouched**, and the check that made that call is worth recording: §5.10 #1 ("Is PCA the same as factor analysis?") looked like a cross-page duplicate until `factor-analysis-pca`'s own three FAQs were read and found to cover keeping components, rotation and loadings, none of them this. Still **291 Q&As**, no duplicate question strings. **software.js: rotated to four untouched by runs 14–16** — `factor-analysis-pca`, `manova`, `post-hoc-tests`, `reliability-and-validity` — against **SPSS 32.0.0 (GA 23 Apr 2026, still current; its additions are the AI Output Assistant, Mediation Analysis, VAR forecasting and a Linux installer, none of which touch Dimension Reduction → Factor, GLM → Multivariate, One-Way ANOVA → Post Hoc or Scale → Reliability)** and **JASP 0.98.1 (7 Jul 2026, still current; the release notes for 0.96 → 0.98.1 document no rename or relocation of EFA/PCA, MANOVA, Post Hoc, Unidimensional Reliability or Inter-Rater Reliability)**. Every statistic re-derived: MANOVA's Pillai .24 gives exact **F(2, 77) = 12.105** against the printed 12.10, its follow-ups **p = .0038 → .004** and **.0822 → .082**; post-hoc **F(3, 116) = 7.21 → p = 1.8e-4** with **η² = .1572 → .16**; §3.10's **χ²(190) = 1438.2** has df exactly 20×19/2 for its 20 variables. **No drift found, so no edits — the honest outcome of a rotation.** Still 34 entries. **Snippets: four extended in step with the new sections** (hierarchical clustering in both languages, `metric="manhattan"`, component scores into `lm()`, and a comment naming `max_iter` as epochs beside the `activation="relu"` default), and **every R line was executed here** on R 4.5.2 before it shipped. **Link health, measured, and a measurement bug worth recording:** the first pass reported eight pages at zero inbound, which was wrong — it compared whole `href`s, so every link carrying a `#fragment` was dropped, and `problems.html` actually has **56** inbound lesson links, not zero. Corrected, **no teaching page sits at 0 inbound**; the six genuine zeroes (`quiz`, `progress`, `toolbox`, `flashcards`, `teachers`, `privacy`) are navigation and utility pages reached from the nav by design, and linking them from lesson prose would be noise. The weakest teaching targets are `which-test.html`, `glossary.html` and `guides/analyze-thesis-data-jasp` at **1 inbound each**, and the weakest lesson is `ethics/why-research-ethics` at **1 outbound** — deliberately left for the run that takes it as a subject rather than fixed by a drive-by link, since none of this run's five subjects can cite them naturally. This run's own prose added three cross-links that did not exist before, including the site's first `data` → `ml` link and the first ML → `multicollinearity` link. **Quips: five reviewed, three kept, two rewritten**, both for the recurring defect of a closing clause that only restates the one before it — §5.9's ended "Clusters are hypotheses, not facts" after the joke had already landed and now lands the lesson's real pivot instead, and §2.10's ended "Anonymity is harder than it looks" after a sentence that had already shown it. **Backlog item — no Backlog section exists (P37 is still unrun), so as in runs 11–16 the item became a checker. Three candidates were built, measured against the corpus, and REJECTED before the fourth worked, which is the part worth recording.** A **stale-forward-promise scanner** (from the "course being built" defect) found 4 sentences, *all* false positives ("as soon as possible", "research is planned"), with the single real instance already fixed. A **cross-page inconsistent-number detector** flagged 2 groups, both false positives, and would not have caught this run's defect anyway: the 87% was **consistently** wrong in all three places, not inconsistently stated. An **eponym roster** was abandoned once the corpus showed the site uses a given name exactly **twice** in 137 pages. The fourth is **`tools/widget-terms.js`**: does a lesson's interactive print a quantity its prose never explains? It reads the string literals a widget writes to `textContent`/`innerHTML` and flags capitalized labels concatenated with a computed value (`"Epoch " + epoch`) that appear nowhere in the lesson's prose. **Two properties are load-bearing and were both learned by testing against the pre-fix tree, not assumed:** widget chrome must be stripped from "prose" (the widget's own `<span>Epoch 0 · untrained</span>` otherwise counts as the lesson explaining it, which is exactly how the defect hid — without the strip the scan reports nothing at all), and **progress counters are excluded by shape rather than by name** (`"Score: " + n + " / " + total` keys on the `" / "` literal, so it generalizes to tallies nobody has written yet). Three broader definitions were measured and discarded first: every word in every rendered literal gave **282** flags of CSS class names and ordinary English, every capitalized word gave **249** dominated by button verbs, and filtering sentence-initial words dropped to 2 but **missed "Epoch"**, which sits at the start of its own literal. It ships with **4 acknowledged-benign flags** across 97 lessons (two example-data values, two self-test tallies) rather than a filter over-fitted to today's corpus, and its blind spot (lowercase labels) is documented in the header instead of smoothed over. **Proved on two independent defect shapes:** deleting the new epoch paragraph from the real tree fired `[FLAG] ml/neural-networks-intuition → Epoch` and exit 1; making `clustering-kmeans`'s widget report an undefined "Inertia" fired that and exit 1; both returned to exit 0 on restore, and `git diff` confirmed no test residue. It stays out of `audit.js` for prose-lint's reason. **Verification:** `audit.js` **0 errors / 20 warn / 64 info** (baseline restored after the index rebuild), `math-check.js` **270 assertions, 0 failures**, `prose-lint --strict` **exit 0**, `faq-audit --strict` **exit 0**, `widget-terms --strict` **exit 0** — with **zero em-dashes added to any subject** (8 / 7 / 7 / 6 / 4, unchanged, while those pages grew by 240–390 words each) and FAQ em-dashes *down* on two lessons. The diff read-back earned its place again, though differently: it surfaced 12 em-dashes in added lines that all proved to be pre-existing text swept up by regenerated FAQ blocks and rewritten paragraph lines, which the per-page counts confirmed. **6 changed pages · 24 inline scripts · 12 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures**; all shared JS, `sw.js`, the five tools and `faq_data.py` parse. Browser pass over all six changed pages via same-origin iframes at **1200px, 375px and 360px**: every expected string present, **0 console errors, 0 leading-slash links, 0 horizontal scroll at any width**, every lesson `h2` carrying an id, **all 7 new/touched link targets returning 200**, the k-NN slider round-tripping exactly (frozen-noise law intact), the XOR widget stepping Epoch 0 → 100, and the privacy demo reproducing **k = 1 / 30 unique → k = 2 / 0 unique**, the very configuration the new 50% claim is computed from. Dark mode verified on three changed lessons (bg `#0b1120`, `--link` `#6f7bf2`, canvases painted) with `localStorage.theme` set before load, per run 16's note. **Index rebuilt (878 KB, 97 lessons, 37 pages) and searched for all 29 terms this run added: 29 HIT, 0 MISS**, each resolving to its own lesson. **`CACHE_VERSION` bumped sc-v35 → sc-v36** (two QUIPS changed and `site.js` is precached; `snippets.js` also changed but is lazy-loaded and never precached). **Three notes for the next run.** The **Ethics foundations are the coherent five** and would finish the Toolkit track. **`apa.html` still emits no confidence interval** — now five runs old, and run 16 made the lesson, its drill and `software.js` all oblige it, so the tool is the last holdout. And run 16's strongest checker candidate is still unbuilt and still looks right: **extending `audit.js` CHECK 9 to page prose and inline scripts**, whose false-positive taxonomy run 16 already worked out; this run did not take it because CHECK 9's defect class was not the class this run found by hand.
  - **27 Jul 2026 — sixteenth run (the Writing write-up spine, the confidence interval the site printed everywhere and taught nowhere, and the end of the hand-written spelling inventory).** The ledger named the thread and this run took it: five of the six never-opened Writing lessons, read as one arc — `imrad-structure` (§3.1), `reporting-statistics-apa` (§3.2), `nonsignificant-results` (§3.5), `discussion-and-limitations` (§3.6), `abstracts-and-titles` (§3.7). **80 of 97 lessons have now been subjects.** **Five absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep, and every published number verified in both `VIZ` and R before it was published.** (1) **The APA confidence interval was the site's largest used-but-untaught term.** `writing/reporting-statistics-apa` contained the string "CI" **zero times**, `cheat-apa.html` zero, `apa.html` zero — while twelve files print `95% CI [...]`, two `checks.js` questions test a student's reading of one, §3.5's key callout calls it "the whole message", and §3.7's abstract-grader rewards the sentence `r = .28, 95% CI [.11, .43]` as its winning answer. Run 13 had already noted §3.2 "never says interval" and left it, since §3.2 was not its subject. §3.2 gained **"Where the confidence interval goes"**: the `95% CI [lower, upper]` form, the level stated once for a series (APA's own example `95% CIs [−7.2, 4.3], [9.2, 12.4], and [−1.2, −0.5]`), the fact that **in a table APA drops the brackets for LL/UL columns with the level in the header** (checked against APA's published sample tables, not memory), the rule nothing on the site taught — **an interval inherits the leading-zero rule of whatever it bounds** — and the reminder to name what the interval is *on*. The worked trio comes from the lesson's own master-patterns table and was computed twice: *d* = 0.96 → **95% CI [0.42, 1.49]** by exact noncentral *t* (bisection on `VIZ.nctCdf`, cross-checked against R's `pt(..., ncp=)` — agreement to 5 dp; the large-sample approximation gives [0.43, 1.49]), *r*(58) = .34 → **[.09, .55]** by Fisher *z*, *b* = 0.42 / *SE* = 0.14 → **[0.14, 0.70]**. One drafted sentence was **wrong and the release notes caught it**: I had written that neither SPSS nor JASP hands you an interval on η², and JASP's ANOVA does, from its effect-size options — the paragraph now says so. (2) **`TOST` and `two one-sided tests` returned zero hits**, on the lesson that names "equivalence testing" as its own idea and stops. §3.5 gained the procedure, run on the lesson's **own two studies**, whose per-group *n* I derived from the published intervals and only afterwards found stated in the lesson's interactive (40/group and 200/group — they matched). Against a bound of ±0.3: the tight null (*d* = 0.05, 200/group) gives **TOST *p* = .006, equivalence declared**; the wide one (*d* = 0.15, 40/group) gives **TOST *p* = .25, nothing declared** — same "not significant", opposite answers. The section also lands the fact everyone gets wrong, with the arithmetic to back it: at α = .05 TOST succeeds exactly when the **90%** interval fits inside the bounds ([−0.11, 0.21] vs [−0.22, 0.52]), because two one-sided tests each spend 5% in one tail. (3) **`common method` and `common-method` returned zero hits**, on the "limitations with teeth" lesson whose own running example is a single-questionnaire self-report correlation. §3.6 gained it, and the algebra was **simulated rather than asserted** (N = 500,000 per cell, agreement to 3 dp): with a shared method factor carrying share *m* of each measure's variance and true correlation ρ, the observed *r* is (1 − *m*)ρ + *m*, so **at ρ = 0 the observed correlation is exactly *m*** — a tenth manufactures *r* = .10 out of nothing, a quarter manufactures *r* = .25. Turned on the lesson's own *r* = .28: a 10% shared method implies a true **.20**, a 25% one implies **.04**. (4) **`JARS`, `Journal Article Reporting Standards` and `reporting standards` returned zero hits**, and nowhere did the site name the Method section's subsections, on the lesson that tells students to write "enough detail that a stranger could repeat the study". §3.1 gained the list, **taken verbatim from APA's own JARS-Quant Table 1 PDF** (inclusion and exclusion, participant characteristics, sampling procedures, sample size/power/precision, measures and covariates, conditions and design, analytic strategy incl. missing data and experiment-wise error) — which also made it the run's biggest cross-link hub, pointing at seven existing lessons and `power.html`. (5) **`structured abstract` returned zero hits**, and nothing said how a keyword line is formatted. §3.7 gained both, the keyword rules quoted from APA's Abstract and Keywords Guide (indented, italic label, three to five terms, lowercase, comma-separated, no closing period). **Four shipped numeric defects, three of them found by a throwaway probe that ran `audit.js` CHECK 9's own pairing logic over two surfaces it does not scan — page prose and inline-script literals.** (a) **`95% CI [.11, .43]` was wrong in four places** (§3.6's result card, §3.7's winning sentence, `checks.js`, `quiz.html`): *r* = .28 at *n* = 120 gives **[.1061, .4373] → [.11, .44]**, confirmed identically by `VIZ` and by R's `cor.test`. (b) §3.2's master-patterns regression row read `b = 0.42, SE = 0.14, β = .29, t(96) = 3.10, p = .002`, but **0.42 ÷ 0.14 = 3.00**, and neither 3.00 nor 3.10 gives *p* = .002 — now `t(96) = 3.00, p = .003`. (c) `writing/final-checklist` reported `t(116) = 2.84, p = .006` where the attainable range is [.00525, .00541] **even after the reader fixes the planted df error to 118** — and *p* was not one of its six planted errors, so an exercise about finding inconsistencies carried an unintended one. Now `.005`. (d) `apa.html`'s static example claimed `t(87) = 3.40, p < .001` where *p* = .00102. Now `p = .001`. **Two more were inside §3.2's own drill**, which nothing checks because CHECK 9 does not read inline scripts: its *corrected* paragraph asserted `t(58) = 3.72, p = .001` (true *p* = .00045) and `r(58) = .34, p < .001` (true *p* = .008). Both fixed by making the drill's own numbers true (`p < 0.001` → `p < .001` as the leading-zero error; the correlation raised to .44, whose *p* = .00044 really is < .001), and the drill **gained an eleventh error teaching the new rule** — `95% CI [0.21, 0.62]` → `[.21, .62]`, verified by Fisher *z*. Driven end-to-end in-browser: 11/11, and the final paragraph is now internally consistent in every statistic. **FAQ freshness: five swaps, scored with run 15's `faq-audit.js` and then judged by hand.** Out: §3.2's leading-zero question (**ECHO 100%** — it restated its own h2 verbatim) and its `p = .000` question (**TWIN 40% question / 27% answer with `writing-results`**); §3.5's "does non-significant mean no effect" (ECHO 50% + the lesson's highest REPRISE at 34%); §3.6's "how many limitations" (two of that lesson's three answers were about limitations and none about the Discussion's other four moves); §3.7's "write it first or last" (ECHO 50% and a near-duplicate of §3.1's answer). In: "What is the difference between N and n?", "My department uses a different style guide. Do these rules still apply?" (**`style guide` had zero hits sitewide**), "I want to claim there is no effect. Equivalence test or Bayes factor?" (JASP's Equivalence T-Tests module confirmed from JASP's own release notes), "My result contradicts a published study. How do I write that?" (the lesson's recipe raises "agree, extend, or contradict" and never answers it), and "Should my abstract mention limitations?". §3.5's surviving first answer also **carried an inconsistent example** — `t(58) = 1.30, p = .20, d = 0.24, 95% CI [−0.13, 0.61]`, where *t* and df give *d* = 0.34 and the interval implies ~57/group — now `d = 0.34, 95% CI [−0.18, 0.84]` (noncentral, R-checked). Still **291 Q&As**, no duplicate question strings. **Two candidate replacements were dropped after grepping rather than shipped**: a post-hoc-power FAQ (already covered in the power lesson, its FAQ and a practice problem) and a Mann–Whitney notation FAQ (`software.js` already shows the form). **software.js:** none of the five subjects has an entry, correctly. Rotated to four not checked in runs 13–15 — `correlation`, `simple-linear-regression`, `one-way-anova`, `chi-square-tests` — against **SPSS 32.0.0 (GA 23 Apr 2026, still current; its additions are the AI Output Assistant, forecasting and a Linux installer, none touching Correlate → Bivariate, Regression → Linear, Compare Means → One-Way ANOVA or Crosstabs)** and **JASP 0.98.1 (7 Jul 2026, still current; the 0.97–0.98.1 notes document no menu renames)**. Their numbers were re-derived and all hold (`r(58) = .42, 95% CI [.19, .61]`; `b/SE = 4.05`, `t² = F = 16.40`, `R² = .25`; χ²(1) = 6.25 → *p* = .012, φ = .23, and its 68%/45% percentages are reachable at N = 120 on an unequal split). Two edits, both obliged by the new §3.2 prose: `one-way-anova`'s JASP step now ticks **Confidence intervals beside the effect-size estimates**, and `simple-linear-regression`'s APA sentence — the site's model regression sentence, which told readers a coefficient has "no excuse" for lacking an interval while lacking one itself — gained **95% CI [1.16, 3.46]** (2.31 ± 2.01063 × 0.57, i.e. *b* ± *t*.975,48 × *SE*, R-verified). Still **34 entries**. **Snippets:** `simple-linear-regression` gained `confint(fit)` and `fit.conf_int()`, the same obligation; the R lines were **executed here** (R 4.5.2) at both the default and 0.99 levels. **Link health, measured.** Inbound counts recounted for all 26 tools, guides and posters: **no content page sits at 0 inbound**, so this run inherited step 7's second question and cleared it. `ml/clustering-kmeans` was the last lesson with **zero outbound prose links** and got three (distance → `knn-and-distance`, within-cluster spread → `one-way-anova`, stability → `cross-validation-and-overfitting`); `stats-1/central-limit-theorem` and `ethics/informed-consent-and-irb` were the last two at one. **All 97 ready lessons now have at least two outbound prose links, and none is a dead end.** **Quips:** five reviewed, three kept, two rewritten — §3.1's had an awkward middle clause that the shortest fix simply deleted, and §3.2's traded a padded "would never, ever" for the interval the lesson now teaches. **Backlog item — no Backlog section exists (P37 is still unrun), so as in runs 11–15 the item became a checker, and this run's own defects defined it.** **`prose-lint.js`'s rule 12 is now generative.** The hand-written `BRIT_SPELLINGS` inventory had failed five times; this run found three more it did not contain — **`editorialise`** (§3.1), **`parenthesised`** (§3.2) and **`unlabelled`** (`clustering-kmeans`), the last of which the inventory *did* hold as `labelled` and still missed, because `\b` does not fire inside a prefixed word. I also caught myself typing **`millimetres`** into the new §3.2 section. The inventory's polarity was backwards: words that legitimately end in `-ise`/`-our` are a small **closed** class, while words that should end in `-ize`/`-or` are **open**. So the two open classes are now matched by shape (`BRIT_GENERATIVE`) with their exceptions allow-listed (`ISE_OK`/`OUR_OK`/`YSE_OK`, the `-aise`/`-oise`/`-uise`/`-wise` families and a prefix group), the inventory keeps only `-re`, doubled consonants, the `-ce` nouns and one-offs, and its 6+-character entries now match as a **suffix**. `-iser`/`-isers` is deliberately unmatched (*Kaiser*, *adviser*, *miser*, *riser*). **The first run of it produced 31 hits; triaging every one gave ten genuine British spellings and four false-positive classes**, and the four allow-list mechanisms exist because the corpus demanded them, not by anticipation: `raises`/`raising` (the `-aise` family needed its inflections), **`Kaiser` ×4** (which is why `-iser` is unmatched), `unsupervised` (prefixes) and `imprecise` (the `im`/`in` prefixes). After the fixes the scan reports **8 → 0**, all of them real: `armour` (a guide), `Industrialised` (the WEIRD acronym in `sampling-methods`), `rigour` (`questionable-research-practices`), `centimetres` (`data-entry-and-validation`, caught by the new suffix rule), `reorganises` (the ML landing page), `personalisation` (**`privacy.html`, in the sentence about GA's consent mode**), `pedestrianise`/`pedestrianisation` (`problems.html`), plus `Dichotomising` in `checks.js` **and again in `quiz.html`**, `symmetrises` and a second `centimetres` in inline scripts. **Proved by reintroducing one defect per surface in the real tree and restoring it**: a coined `-ise` verb in a lesson, an inventory word behind a prefix, a `-our` noun in a `checks.js` string, an `-isation` noun entering through `faq_data.py` (which surfaced against its own lesson page, confirming the FAQ path), and a `-re` form inside a `snippets.js` comment — **five hits, one per surface, exit 1, and exit 0 on restore.** VOICE.md rule 12 and CLAUDE.md were rewritten in the same commit, as both files require. **Verification:** `audit.js` **0 errors / 20 warn / 64 info** (baseline restored after the index rebuild), `math-check.js` **270 assertions, 0 failures**, `prose-lint --strict` **exit 0**, `faq-audit --strict` **exit 0** — with **zero em-dashes added to any subject** (2 / 5 / 5 / 7 / 5, one *fewer* than before on §3.2, and FAQ em-dashes down on two lessons). **20 changed pages · 78 inline scripts · 35 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures**; all shared JS, `sw.js`, the five tools and `faq_data.py` parse. Browser pass over all 20 changed pages via same-origin iframes at **1200px, 375px and 360px**: every expected string present, **0 console errors, 0 leading-slash links, 0 horizontal scroll at any width**, every lesson `h2` carrying an id, **all 22 new link targets returning 200**, the three interactives whose data changed still driving correctly, and dark mode verified on three changed lessons (bg `#0b1120`, `--link` `#6f7bf2`). One methodological note for future browser passes: flipping `data-theme` on an **offscreen** iframe updates the CSS variable but does not repaint the transitioned `color`, which reads as a false dark-mode regression — set `localStorage.theme` before load instead. **Index rebuilt (868 KB, 97 lessons, 37 pages) and searched for all 27 terms this run added: 27 HIT, 0 MISS**, each resolving to the right lesson. **`CACHE_VERSION` bumped sc-v34 → sc-v35** (two QUIPS changed and `site.js` is precached; nothing else in the shell moved). **Three notes for the next run.** The strongest checker candidate is now measured rather than guessed: **extending CHECK 9 to page prose finds real defects** (three of this run's four), and its false-positive taxonomy is already worked out — compare the printed *p* against the range implied by the **statistic's own rounding precision** (that alone cleared four of five rounding false positives), skip **arithmetic-chain lines** of the shape `F(1, 56) = 540 / 90 = 6.00` (the parser grabs the first number), and give **deliberately-planted-error pages an opt-out**, since `final-checklist`'s exercise and §3.2's drill are *supposed* to contain wrong statistics; the inline-script surface is the dangerous one for exactly that reason. `apa.html` still emits no confidence interval — now four runs old, and this run has made the lesson, the drill and `software.js` all oblige it, so the tool is the last holdout. And `flexRe` still cannot match a period, so `requirements.txt` remains unsearchable (run 14's finding, still a P71 session's job).
  - **27 Jul 2026 — fifteenth run (the ML evaluation spine, and the first tool that measures what step 4 has always done by eye).** The ledger named the thread and this run took it: five of the nine never-opened ML lessons, read as one arc rather than a scattered five — `prediction-vs-explanation` (§5.1), `regularization-ridge-and-lasso` (§5.3), `classification-metrics` (§5.4), `roc-curves-and-auc` (§5.5), `random-forests-and-ensembles` (§5.7). **75 of 97 lessons have now been subjects.** **Five absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep, and every published number verified before it was published.** (1) **`Table 2 fallacy` returned zero hits sitewide**, on the lesson whose own FAQ warns against reading a predictor's internals as causal effects and whose closing line is "the danger is doing one while silently believing you're doing the other". §5.1 gained the named version, built on a DGP checked algebraically and by simulation at n = 200,000: with Z confounding X→Y (Z→X 0.6, X→Y 0.5, Z→Y 0.4), the adjusted model recovers **bX = 0.500** exactly as intended while handing back **bZ = 0.400** for a variable whose *total* effect is **0.70** — the table understates motivation by 43% — and the unadjusted X slope reads **0.676**, matching the closed form .92/1.36 = **0.6765**. The section's point is that adjustment buys one interpretable row and turns every other into a direct effect, which makes it a question about the causal diagram, not the output. (2) **`post-selection inference` and `selective inference` returned zero hits**, on the lesson that teaches lasso as automatic variable selection, on a site otherwise full of p-values. §5.3 gained it, and the number was simulated rather than asserted: a coordinate-descent lasso **validated two ways first** (λ = 0 reproduces `lm()` to 5 dp; the all-zero threshold equals max|x′y|/n exactly), then run on **1,200 replicates of 200 rows × 40 pure-noise predictors**. At λ = 0.60 λmax lasso keeps about six predictors *out of nothing at all*, and refitting those six by least squares returns **p < .05 for 32.8% of their coefficients** (7,293 coefficients; median p = .084) against a nominal 5%. The control settles it: the same count of predictors chosen **before** anyone looked at the outcome gives **4.7%** and a median p of **.488**. So the inflation is the selection, not the arithmetic or my code. (3) **`balanced accuracy` and `Matthews` both returned zero hits**, and **`negative predictive value` zero**, while `positive predictive value` appeared exactly **once** sitewide (a passing clause in `stats-1/probability-basics`'s FAQ, never once connected to "precision") and `PPV`/`NPV` only inside `problems.html`'s `<!-- verify -->` comment. §5.4 gained both halves: the clinical-vocabulary bridge (precision = PPV, its mirror TN/(TN+FN) = NPV, with the pointer to Bayes' rule) and a section on the two summaries a rare class cannot fool, run through the lesson's own 5%-prevalence example — the all-negative parrot scores **95% accuracy, 50% balanced accuracy, MCC 0**, while a real model at TP 30 / FN 20 / FP 50 / TN 900 scores **93% accuracy** (lower!), **77.4% balanced accuracy, MCC .44**. MCC was confirmed to be literally the Pearson correlation of the two 0/1 vectors (both **0.439732**), and the section says honestly that the parrot's MCC is a **convention** (zero denominator), not a computed correlation. (4) **`Youden` returned zero hits and `calibration` only as *calibration of claims*** (the Writing course) or a miscalibrated survey item; `Brier` was absent entirely. §5.5 gained both. Youden's J went into the existing "Reading the curve" section with the analytic result that for two equal-variance normals the maximizer is always the midpoint (dJ/dt = 0 ⟹ t = d/2), so at *d* = 1 it sits at threshold 0.50 with **TPR .6915, FPR .3085, J = .3829** — which are the viz's own default slider values, so a reader can check it on the plot. Calibration got its own section on the fact that **AUC reads only the ordering**: 100 patients with 20 events, a model scoring .55/.45 ranks perfectly at **AUC 1.00 and Brier .2025**, while a model saying "20%" to everyone ranks nothing at **AUC 0.50 and Brier .160** — the useless model wins — and recalibrating the perfect ranker to .95/.05 drops Brier to **.0025** with AUC unmoved. The benchmark named is *p̄*(1 − *p̄*) = .160. (5) **`boosting` returned zero hits as a method** (every hit was the ordinary English verb), and `gradient boosting`, `XGBoost` and `AdaBoost` zero — in a lesson titled "Random Forests **& Ensembles**" that teaches bagging only. §5.7 gained boosting, and the claim that separates it from a forest was **simulated, not repeated**: hand-rolled gradient boosting (depth-3 `rpart` trees, shrinkage 0.3) and bagged deep trees on the *same* 200 rows with 15% label noise, scored on 6,000 test cases. The forest's test error falls **.323 → .189 by fifteen trees and sits at .190 at 300**; the boosted model **bottoms out at .184 after five trees and climbs to .242 by 500, 32% worse than its own best**, having hit **100% training accuracy at tree 43**. A shrinkage sweep supplied the honest qualifier: the minimum arrives at tree **5 / 16 / 59** for rates 0.3 / 0.1 / 0.03 and every one of them turns upward, so shrinkage buys time and not immunity. Also **`partial dependence` appeared exactly once sitewide, in §5.7's own callout, never taught** — now explained in the importance section (sweep one predictor, average the prediction over the others as they actually occur). **FAQ freshness: five swaps, one per lesson, each displacing a question that restated its own lesson's title or an h2**, each duplicate-checked against all 291 first. Out: "What is the difference between prediction and explanation?" (the lesson title), "Ridge or lasso — which should I use?" (its h2), "What is the difference between precision and recall?" (the lesson's own bullet list, verbatim), "When should I use a precision-recall curve instead of ROC?" (its h2), "What is out-of-bag (OOB) error?" (its h2). In: "Can I do machine learning with only 80 participants?" (verified — 16 of 20 test cases is 80% with a Wilson interval of **[.584, .919]**, cross-checked against the exact binomial [.563, .943]), "Does regularization work for logistic regression too?" (**`penalized logistic` had zero hits**; the answer's sharpest fact is that scikit-learn's `LogisticRegression` is **already L2-penalized at C = 1**, so the unpenalized fit is the thing you must request), "My classes are very imbalanced. Should I oversample the rare class?" (**SMOTE / oversampling / class weights: zero hits sitewide**), "Model A's AUC is .82 and model B's is .79. Is A actually better?" (**`DeLong` zero hits**; `roc.test`'s paired default confirmed from pROC's documentation), and "Do I need to tune a random forest, or are the defaults good enough?" (**`mtry` existed only in a code comment and a snippet**; scikit-learn's own docs supplied the trap — `RandomForestRegressor` ships `max_features=1.0`, which the documentation itself calls "equivalent to bagged trees"). Still **291 Q&As**, no duplicate question strings. **software.js:** none of the five subjects has an entry (correct — no SPSS/JASP path exists for them). Rotated four not checked in runs 12–14 — `mixed-and-multilevel-models`, `missing-data`, `bayesian-estimation`, `generalized-linear-models` — against **SPSS 32.0.0** and **JASP 0.98.1 (7 Jul 2026, still the current release, no menu renames in its notes)**; SPSS 32's additions are Mediation Analysis, VAR models, an AI Output Assistant and passkeys, none of which touch those four paths, and `missing-data`'s claim that JASP still has no imputation module was re-confirmed (it remains an open feature request). **No step changed.** The one obligation the new prose created went to `logistic-regression`, the site's only classification walkthrough: **`Classification Table` / `cut value` / `classification cutoff` had zero hits sitewide**, so a step now names SPSS's default table and its fixed .500 cutoff, and a tip explains that its *Overall Percentage* is plain accuracy and flatters a rare outcome. Still **34 entries**. **Snippets:** four extended in step with the new sections (balanced accuracy + MCC, Brier + calibration curve + Youden coords + paired `roc.test`, partial dependence + `HistGradientBoostingClassifier`, and a comment on the lasso block warning against refitting the survivors for p-values). **caret, pROC, glmnet, randomForest and gbm are not installed on this machine**, so those R lines were verified against the packages' own documentation rather than executed, and the note says so plainly; every R line this run *could* run — the whole lasso validation, the boosting and bagging simulations, the shrinkage sweep, the Table 2 DGP and both proportion intervals — was executed here on R 4.5.2 with rpart 4.1.24. **Link health, measured:** inbound lesson links recounted for all 26 tools, guides and posters. `cheat-test-chooser.html` was the last content page at **0 inbound**, exactly as run 14 predicted; it now has five, each matched to a panel the poster actually owns (§1.1 types-of-data → the outcome-type question the whole map starts from, §2.1 one-way-anova → panel ①, §2.5 non-parametric-alternatives → panel ②, §2.6 chi-square-tests → panel ③, §3.1 multiple-regression → panel ④). **No content page is left at 0 inbound**, so the next run inherits step 7's second question: the lessons at 0–1 outbound prose links are `ml/clustering-kmeans`, `data/data-privacy-basics` and `writing/abstracts-and-titles` at zero, then `stats-1/central-limit-theorem`, `data/merging-datasets` and `ethics/informed-consent-and-irb` at one. **Quips:** five reviewed, two kept, three rewritten. All three had the recurring defect (a closing clause that only restates the opener), and §5.3's also carried a **"quietly"** — VOICE.md rule 9's banned intensifier, sitting in the one surface `prose-lint --strict` holds to rule 12 only. **Backlog item — no Backlog section exists (P37 is still unrun), so as in runs 11–14 the item became a checker, and this run's own hand-work defined it.** `tools/faq-audit.js` (zero-dependency, Node built-ins) measures the three inputs to step 4 that fifteen runs have judged by eye: **ECHO** (question content words contained in one of its own lesson's headings), **REPRISE** (share of the answer's 4-word shingles already verbatim in the lesson body — CLAUDE.md's "complement, don't repeat" with a number on it), and **TWIN** (nearest question and nearest answer on any other lesson, the criterion that caught run 13's two duplicates). It also prints the **opener-template census** PROMPTS.md flags as unmeasured: **52 of 291 "what-is", 24 "what-is-the-difference", 41 "how", 38 "is/does", 23 "why", 19 "can-i"**, with 52 in "other" — the buckets are exclusive and the difference-template one is tested first, so PROMPTS.md's count of 24 is confirmed exactly while its "59 with What is" corresponds to a wider bucket than this tool's. **It was calibrated against the tree BEFORE this run's swaps, and the calibration is reported honestly because it is partly negative:** four of the five questions already marked for replacement came back as the highest-ECHO question on their own lesson (67%, 100%, 50%, 67%; two inside the sitewide top 12), but the fifth — `classification-metrics`'s precision-vs-recall question — scored **ECHO 0% and REPRISE 0%** and the tool missed it entirely, because it restates a **bullet list** rather than a heading and paraphrases instead of copying. That boundary is documented in the script header and in CLAUDE.md rather than smoothed over. **`--strict` gates structural faults only** (not exactly 3 per lesson, a slug with no ready lesson, two identical question strings), because the honest verdict on a high ECHO is often "leave it" and a gated threshold would just teach future runs to write around the metric; it stays out of `audit.js` for prose-lint's reason. Proved by reintroducing both structural defects in a throwaway worktree at HEAD: a fourth Q&A fired `random-forests-and-ensembles: 4 Q&As (must be exactly 3)` and exit 1, a duplicated question string fired `identical question on train-test-split-and-generalization and roc-curves-and-auc` and exit 1, and both returned to exit 0 on restore. **Verification:** `audit.js` **0 errors / 20 warn / 64 info**, `math-check.js` **270 assertions, 0 failures**, `prose-lint --strict` **exit 0**, `faq-audit --strict` **exit 0** — with **zero em-dashes added to any of the five subjects** (7 / 9 / 9 / 9 / 9, unchanged), which mattered because four of them were already sitting at 9 against a budget of 10. **137 pages · 534 inline scripts · 235 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures** sitewide (10 changed pages · 42 · 20); all shared JS, `sw.js`, the four tools and `faq_data.py` parse.   - **27 Jul 2026 — fourteenth run (the Data course's raw-file-to-analysis-ready spine, and the largest unlinted prose surface on the site).** First run under the rewritten prompt, so selection came from the ledger rather than from git dates: five of the six Data lessons no run had opened, taken as one thread rather than a scattered five — `data-cleaning-workflow` (§2.4), `outliers-in-practice` (§2.5), `transformations-and-recoding` (§2.6), `merging-datasets` (§2.8), `reproducible-workflows` (§2.9). **70 of 97 lessons have now been subjects, and the Data course is down to its last one.** **Four absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep rather than assumed, and one shipped defect.** (1) **`median absolute deviation` and `MAD` returned zero hits sitewide**, on the lesson that states the masking problem in its own z-score bullet ("a lone giant can hide by inflating the SD") and then offers no robust estimator. §2.5 gained the section, built on two verified facts: the largest standardized score possible in a sample of size *n* is (*n*−1)/√*n*, so **at *n* = 10 the ceiling is 2.846 and |z| > 3 can never fire** (confirmed by construction at nine sample sizes and by a 200,000-sample random search that never beat the bound), and a worked twenty-age example where two typos hide each other — **z(511) = 2.97 and z(498) = 2.88, so neither is flagged**, while median 23 and MAD 3 give **robust scores of 110 and 107** and the oldest genuine student (34) sits at 2.47. The 1.5 × IQR fence catches both at 37, so the new section reinforces the existing advice instead of contradicting it. (2) **`Box-Cox` returned zero hits sitewide**, on a transformations lesson offering log and √ with nothing to choose between them. §2.6 gained the ladder of powers with a verified skewness table (λ = 1 → 4.74, 0.5 → 1.60, 0 → −0.03, −1 → **−5.51, overshot into left skew** — the "stronger squeeze" framing hides that you can go too far) plus λ̂ recovery checked **two independent ways**, a hand-rolled profile likelihood and `MASS::boxcox`, agreeing to 3 dp on all five test cases. **The honest finding was simulated rather than asserted:** over 2,000 right-skewed samples at *n* = 60 the λ interval has median width **0.50 and never once contains 1**, while on already-normal samples of the same size it is **2.33 wide and contains 1 in 97% of runs** — so Box–Cox is decisive about *whether* to transform and vague about which rung, which is why the prose says round λ̂ to a readable rung rather than report 0.407. (3) **`anti-join` returned zero hits**, and `relationship =` none either, on the lesson whose entire "two habits that save you" section is about join shape. §2.8 gained the assertive form, and the sharpest fact came out of running it: **`dplyr` 1.2.0 warns on a many-to-many join and says nothing at all about a one-to-many** — the lesson's own `sessions` table is one-to-many, so its four participants silently return five rows. `relationship = "one-to-one"` errors; `anti_join` names Ann, Dan and the orphaned session 5. (4) **`sessionInfo`, `renv`, `requirements.txt` and `README` all returned zero hits sitewide**, on the lesson whose own FAQ says "the software and package versions are recorded" without ever saying how. §2.9 gained the section, anchored on a claim I checked rather than repeated: **R changed how `sample()` draws from a seeded stream in 3.6.0, so `set.seed(42); sample(1:10)` returns `1 5 10 8 2 4 6 9 7 3` on one side of that release and `10 9 3 6 4 8 5 1 2 7` on the other** (`rnorm` untouched, which is why it stays hidden). That lesson's FAQ 2 claimed a seed reproduces "on every run and every machine", which the new section falsifies; it now says "every run of the same software". **The shipped defect was in §2.4's own snippet.** Its R half called bare `distinct()` while its FAQ correctly says to define uniqueness by key — and on the lesson's own scenario those disagree: 7 rows and 5 participants where one resubmission carries a two-minute-later timestamp gives **6 rows and a mean of 65.83 from `distinct()` against the correct 5 rows and 67.4**. Both halves now de-duplicate on the key, and §2.4 gained a short section showing the case, since the lesson previously only ever demonstrated *exact* duplicates. Also §2.4: **`standardise_group()` in the visible cleaning log** (fixed, and it became this run's checker), ISO 8601 named in the date-drift bullet, and the "impossible values" bullet now hands declared missing-value codes to §2.2, which already teaches them. **FAQ freshness: five swaps, each displacing an answer that restated its own lesson's h2** and each duplicate-checked against all 291 first. §2.4's spreadsheet-vs-script answer also duplicated §2.9's whole lesson; §2.8's was one of the 24 "What's the difference between X and Y?" template questions the prompt flags. What shipped: "Two rows share an ID but give different answers. Which one do I keep?", "How many outliers is too many?" (simulated: the 1.5 × IQR fences sit at **z = ±2.698 → 0.70%** of a normal by construction, and **a perfectly normal sample of 200 gets 1.7 flags on average with 75% of samples flagging at least one** — so two flagged values in 200 people is the expected result, not a finding), "I analyzed my outcome on the log scale.   - **27 Jul 2026 — thirteenth run (the two courses no run had ever opened, and the discovery that a quarter of the site's prose was unsearchable).** The staleness ranking put `ml/decision-trees` first by six days (7 Jul, untouched since P15 built it) and then hit a flat 13 Jul tier of 26 files left over from the P43–P47 voice passes, so the tie was broken toward **the two courses no P39 run had ever taken as a subject**, which run 12's note had flagged: `ml/train-test-split-and-generalization` (§5.2), `ml/llms-and-ai-in-research` (§5.12), `writing/writing-results` (§3.4) and `writing/tables-and-figures` (§3.3). **65 of 97 lessons have now been subjects.** **Six absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep rather than assumed.** (1) **`information gain` returned zero hits sitewide** and `entropy` only one, in `neural-networks-intuition`'s cross-entropy loss, on the lesson that teaches splitting; a student reading scikit-learn's `criterion=` or any textbook's ID3/C4.5 section had nowhere to land. §5.6 gained it with a worked split (parent Gini .5 and entropy 1 bit; the split leaves weighted Gini **.3125** for a drop of **.1875**, weighted entropy **.704** for a gain of **.296 bits**) and the honest comparison, simulated rather than asserted: over **4,000 datasets with all 199 thresholds enumerated, the best Gini split and the best information-gain split were the identical threshold 85% of the time**. (2) **`cost-complexity` returned zero hits**, while the lesson body and its own FAQ both told readers to prune without ever saying what pruning optimizes. §5.6 gained *R*(T) + α × leaves and the worked threshold: a four-leaf subtree missing 6 of 200 against a collapsed leaf missing 14 survives only while **α < (.07 − .03) ÷ 3 = .0133**. Both R halves were **executed here** (R 4.5.2, rpart 4.1.24): `split = "gini"` and `split = "information"` both fit, and `printcp`/`prune` reproduce the nested sequence, with rpart's `cp` scaled to the root error exactly as the new prose says. (3) **Accuracy had no uncertainty anywhere on the site.** No lesson, tool or guide put a confidence interval on a test score, and `Wilson` returned zero hits, on a *statistics* site whose ML track reports accuracy nine times. §5.2 gained "Your test score is an estimate too": **.86 on 200 test cases → [.805, .901]**, the same .86 on 50 → **[.738, .930]**, and **±5 points needs ~185 cases while ±3 needs ~514**. Every figure was recomputed in R (`prop.test(..., correct = FALSE)`) and cross-checked against the exact `binom.test` ([.804, .905] and [.733, .942]), and **McNemar's test is named for the first time on the site** (it previously appeared only in `cheat-assumptions.html`). (4) **`retrieval` and `knowledge cutoff` returned zero hits**, on the lesson about LLMs, whose mechanism paragraph said flatly that there is "no lookup" — true of a bare model, not of the tools a 2026 student actually opens. §5.12 gained a section on both, and the mechanism sentence gained one word ("no **built-in** lookup"). It is deliberately evergreen: no product names, no version numbers, and the point it lands is that **a live link is evidence the source exists, never that the sentence is true**. (5) **The confidence interval was missing from the lesson that teaches the results-sentence formula.** `writing/writing-results` contained the string "CI" zero times and `writing/reporting-statistics-apa` never says "interval", while `software.js` injects **95% CI into 16 model APA sentences** across the site. The formula went from four moves to five, and the worked example was recomputed from its own numbers before the interval was added (pooled SD 5.2521, SE 1.3561 → *t* = 3.1709, *p* = .00243, *d* = 0.8187, *t*.975,58 = 2.00172 → **95% CI [1.59, 7.01]**). The lesson's own drill was extended to match, since a five-part formula drilled in four parts teaches the wrong thing: a seventh good fragment, the CI column added to the mock output, and **a new trap on APA's square brackets** (verified in-browser: 7/7 assembles the full sentence, and picking `(1.59, 7.01)` fires the bracket note). (6) **Figure caption placement returned zero hits sitewide**, so §3.3 gained "Figures have that same anatomy now": number bold above, italic title, note below, and the APA-6 habit that leaves a thesis written half in each edition. **FAQ freshness: six swaps, not five, because two of one lesson's three duplicated another page.** `llms-and-ai-in-research` asked "Why do LLMs make up references?" and "Do I have to disclose that I used AI?" while `ethics/ai-in-research-ethics` already owned both almost verbatim, so both were replaced (the retrieval-era "the AI gave me a real citation with a working link, is the claim safe now?", and "does telling the model *don't make anything up* actually help?", answered from the mechanism rather than from folklore). The other four each displaced an answer restating its own lesson's h2: regression trees (zero hits sitewide, the step-function and no-extrapolation consequences), "my test accuracy came out higher than my training accuracy", "how do I report participants who dropped out or were excluded" (the question §3.4 gestured at in its closing line and never answered), and "can I paste my SPSS output tables straight into my thesis". Still **291 Q&As**; FAQ em-dashes fell on four of the five subjects. **software.js:** the version note these steps carried was stale — **JASP is at 0.98.1 (7 Jul 2026), four minor releases past the 0.19.x of earlier runs** — so the 0.96/0.97/0.98 release notes were read for renames across the four walkthroughs re-verified (`factor-analysis-pca`, `manova`, `logistic-regression`, `meta-analysis`). **No menu was renamed**; the one real drift is 0.96's **online module library**, so the meta-analysis JASP step now says how a module is actually installed. One step was added to `independent-samples-t-test` pointing at SPSS's *95% Confidence Interval of the Difference* column, which §3.4's new fifth move obliges. Still **34 entries**. **Snippets:** seven British spellings in comments corrected, two `#`-comment additions plus two runnable lines, and **every added R line was executed on this machine**; the two Python additions (`proportion_confint`, `cost_complexity_pruning_path`) could not be, since this machine still has no scientific Python. **Cross-pollination — the orphan was `formulas.html`**, which run 12 named and left: **0 inbound links from all 97 lessons**, now five, each a genuine formula↔lesson match (§1.4 mean/variance/SD, §1.9 the three t-formulas, §2.2 the ANOVA bookkeeping, §2.5 the four χ² rows, §3.6 the log-odds pair). `guides/choose-statistics-dissertation` remains the last 0-inbound orphan. **Quips:** five reviewed, two kept, three rewritten — all three had a second clause that only restated the first, the defect runs 9, 11 and 12 each fixed elsewhere. **Backlog item — no Backlog section exists (P37 is still unrun), so as in runs 11 and 12 the item became a checker, and this time runs 11 and 12 had already named the target in their own process notes: the JS-injected strings, "the site's one unlinted prose surface".** `prose-lint.js` now scans **2,638 strings across four surfaces** at two deliberate strictness levels: `checks.js` and `software.js` get the **full budget-0 rule set** (ordinary site prose that happens to live in a `.js` file), while `snippets.js`'s `#` comments and `site.js`'s `QUIPS` are held to **rule 12 alone** (a code comment is an annotation, quips are exempt brand voice, and spelling is neither voice nor judgment). Em-dashes there are reported and not gated, because rule 1 is per *page* and none of these are pages; `--page assets/js/software.js` inspects a surface the way `--page <lesson>` inspects a lesson. **It found 8 real defects on its first run** (seven British spellings in snippet comments, one contrast punch in a `checks.js` "why"), all fixed, and it was proved by reintroducing one defect of each class on each surface — all four fired, and `--strict` returned to 0 on restore. VOICE.md, CLAUDE.md and AGENTS.md were corrected in the same commit, since all three stated the opposite scope. **The run's largest find came from following run 12's own closing advice: search the rebuilt index for the terms you just added.** Four came back MISS — not because the words were unwritten but because **`MAX_CHARS = 4500` was truncating 87 of the 97 lessons and dropping 27% of the site's lesson prose (154 KB)**, and because the "Common questions" block sits at the END of an article, the cut landed almost exactly on the FAQ answers. **The site's own search could not find a single one of the 291 answers that are baked into HTML precisely so they can be found.** Fixed with a `LESSON_MAX_CHARS` of 12,000 against a longest lesson of 10,393, the same reasoning the `problems.html` and `glossary.html` overrides already carry. The index goes **698 KB → 856 KB** and stays lazy-loaded; a full-corpus regex pass measures **0.93 ms over 97 documents**, so P71's finding that no debounce is needed still holds. All fourteen terms this run added now resolve to the right lesson. **Verification:** `audit.js` **0 errors / 21 warn / 64 info**, `math-check.js` **270 assertions, 0 failures**, `prose-lint --strict` **exit 0** with **zero em-dashes added to any of the ten changed pages** (§3.4 in fact fell 6 → 4). **10 changed pages · 40 inline scripts · 20 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures**; all shared JS, `sw.js`, the four tools and `faq_data.py` parse. Browser pass over the ten via same-origin iframes at **1200px and 375px**: every expected new string present, **0 console errors, 0 leading-slash links, 0 horizontal scroll at either width**, every h2 carrying an id for the mini-TOC and share-links, all six new link targets returning 200, dark mode verified (bg #0b1120, `--link` #6f7bf2), the injected SPSS and JASP boxes both carrying their new steps, and the search overlay loading the larger index and returning `decision-trees` for *information gain*. **`CACHE_VERSION` bumped sc-v30 → sc-v31** (three QUIPS changed and `site.js` is precached). **Two notes for the next run.** `apa.html`, the APA Results Formatter, **emits no confidence interval at all**, which is now out of step with §3.4's five-move formula and with the 16 model sentences in `software.js`; that is a tool gap, not a content gap, and belongs to a session that can rework the tool. And I wrote "quietly" into §3.3 and caught it reading back — **the seventh consecutive run to trip on VOICE.md's easiest rule** — though from this run forward that class of miss is at least measured everywhere except the page prose I was writing at the time.
  - **27 Jul 2026 — twelfth run (the Stats 2 correlation/regression bridge, and the first check on the site's own APA sentences).** The staleness ranking (last commit that substantively changed this file, mass passes excluded) cleared to five lessons no run had touched: `stats-2/correlation` (§2.8), `stats-2/simple-linear-regression` (§2.9), `stats-3/multicollinearity-and-variable-selection` (§3.2), `methods/reliability-and-validity` (§1.3, 4 Jul) and `ethics/deception-and-debriefing` (§4.3, 6 Jul) — **the first run to reach Stats 2's correlation/regression spine**, and 60 of 97 lessons have now been a P39 subject, leaving 37 — of which 20 are the two entirely untouched courses, ML & AI (12) and Writing (8). **Five absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep rather than assumed.** (1) **Restriction of range.** Its only hit sitewide was `quiz.html`, which *tests* it ("compute r using only high-GPA students → weakened") while **no lesson taught it** — a student who gets that question wrong had nowhere to go. §2.8 gained a section; Thorndike's Case II was checked against a 400,000-draw simulation, ρ = .60 falling to **.3452 in the top quartile of x** (formula .3457) and **.2952 in the top decile** (.2936). (2) **Attenuation from unreliability** had zero teaching hits, and it is the bridge between two lessons in this batch. Both ends now carry it: r_obs = r_true·√(r_xx·r_yy) verified by simulation (**.4211 observed against .4200 predicted** at ρ = .60, both reliabilities .70), plus the ceiling that two scales at α = .70 cannot report above .70 however tight the true relationship. (3) **Regression to the mean** appeared only as a *wrong answer* in two quiz distractors and one passing phrase in §4.7's shrinkage callout — never taught, on the lesson **named after it**. §2.9 gained "Where the name comes from" (Galton 1886): standardizing makes the least-squares slope exactly *r*, so at a retest r of .70 a group scoring 2 SD up averages **1.4 SD** on the retest (2M-draw simulation 1.3998 against theory 1.4000) and **80.0% of them score lower the second time** (Φ(0.8402) = .7995), which is why "we tutored the lowest scorers and they improved" proves little. (4) **Tolerance.** `problems.html` Problem 29 makes the student *compute* it, `software.js` tells SPSS users to read the column and its APA sentence reports "all tolerances ≥ .43", while §3.2 defined VIF and never tolerance. Now named, with VIF > 5 ⇔ tolerance < .20. (5) **Cohen's κ returned zero hits sitewide**, on a lesson that names inter-rater reliability and gives a statistic only for internal consistency. §1.3 gained it, built on the base-rate pair: two coders at an identical p_o = .90 earn **κ = .80** when labels are balanced and **κ = .44** when one label covers 85 of 100 clips. **The run also found a defect in shipped content, which is what item 3 was for.** `software.js`'s `simple-linear-regression` APA sentence was internally inconsistent: b = 2.31 and SE = 0.57 give t(48) = 4.05 and F(1, 48) = 16.40, hence **R² = .2547 and β = .5049**, while the sentence printed **R² = .21, β = .45** and "a fifth of the variance". Corrected to .25 / .50 / "a quarter". A second, smaller one: `repeated-measures-anova` printed p = .002 where its Greenhouse–Geisser F(1.56, 45.2) = 8.75 gives .0015 (ηp² = .23 re-verified). **Backlog item — there is no Backlog section (P37 is still unrun), so as in run 11 the item became a checker, and this time the run's own defect defined it.** `audit.js` gained **CHECK 9**: it loads `viz.js` into the audit's existing bare sandbox (no DOM shim needed) and recomputes p from every t/F/χ²/z/r printed in a `software.js` `apa` string or a baked `.apa-quote` block — the statcheck test `apa.html` already offers readers, turned on the site's own content. **27 pairs, 0 errors**, and it was proved by reintroducing both bugs (caught both) before restoring. Its one design constraint is documented in CLAUDE.md and worth keeping: pairing must be **clause-local** (same clause, ≤ 80 chars, no intervening statistic), because a greedy pairing reports **eight false mismatches** on this very corpus — an APA sentence routinely runs "…, p = .011, ηp² = .08: … F(1, 76) = 15.2, p < .001". **FAQ freshness: four swaps, not five.** §2.8's three were checked against all 291 and none restated the lesson or duplicated another page (its r benchmarks and `effect-size-and-power`'s d benchmarks are complementary), and all three are strong queries, so **removing one would have been a net loss** — the gap it would have filled, **partial correlation (zero hits sitewide)**, was closed with one prose clause instead. The four that shipped each displaced an answer restating its own lesson: "How do I know whether my slope is statistically significant?" (t = b/SE on df = n − 2, the CI [1.16, 3.46] from t.975,48 = 2.0106, and the two identities that let a reader self-check — t² is the model F, and the slope test is the r test), "Every pairwise correlation is below .6. Am I safe from multicollinearity?" (four predictors where the fourth is the sum of the first three: **every pairwise r ≈ .55 while VIF = 13 and tolerance = .08**, exact and simulated, and the reason VIF exists at all), "What counts as a good Cohen's κ?", and "Is not telling participants my hypothesis the same as deception?" (incomplete disclosure vs deception — the question the lesson gestured at twice and never answered). Still **291 Q&As**; FAQ em-dashes down on two pages. **A voice observation for a future pass:** 33 FAQ questions across the site open with the identical "What is the difference between X and Y?" template, which no linter measures. **software.js:** the batch's three walkthroughs re-verified against SPSS 29–31 and JASP 0.19.x menu reality, **no step changed**; two steps added for the new κ prose (SPSS Crosstabs → Statistics → Kappa, with the same-category-codes gotcha that silently breaks the square table; JASP's Reliability → Inter-Rater Reliability) and four tips. Still **34 entries**. **Snippets:** three extended, and **every added R line was executed on this machine** (R 4.5.2, car 3.1.5) — base-R κ returns exactly 0.8 on the lesson's own balanced example, partial correlation takes the ice-cream link from .44 to −.025 once temperature is removed, and `1 / car::vif(fit)` returns tolerance .073 at VIF 13.6. `psych`, `irr` and `ppcor` are **not** installed here, which is why the κ snippet was written in **base R**: no new dependency and actually runnable. The two Python additions (`pingouin.partial_corr`, `sklearn.cohen_kappa_score`) could **not** be executed — this machine has no scientific Python at all. **Cross-pollination — the orphan was `datasets.html`**, which run 11 named and did not fix: it had **0 inbound links from all 97 lessons**. Five went in, each a real dataset↔lesson match rather than a decoration (§2.8 → screen-time, §1.3 → wellbeing-survey, §1.12 → sleep-experiment, §2.1 → study-methods, §3.7 → admissions), with every row count and column name checked against the shipped CSVs — which corrected one claim in the draft (wellbeing-survey has **two** reverse-worded items, q3 and q6, not one). Next orphans, deliberately left: `guides/choose-statistics-dissertation` and `formulas.html`, both at 0 inbound. **Quips:** five reviewed, three kept, two rewritten — multicollinearity's second sentence explained its own first (the exact defect runs 9 and 11 fixed elsewhere) and is now just "Two predictors sharing one trench coat and pretending to be different variables"; §2.9's flat "— and being right" tag became "pick the line that regrets the least". **Verification:** `audit.js` **0 errors / 21 warn / 64 info** (63 + CHECK 9's pair-count line), `math-check.js` **270 assertions, 0 failures**, `prose-lint --strict` **exit 0** with **zero em-dashes added to any of the eight changed pages** (9 / 8 / 4 / 4 / 8 unchanged on the five subjects). **8 changed pages · 34 inline scripts · 16 JSON-LD blocks, 0 failures**; sitewide **235 JSON-LD blocks and 534 inline scripts** parse; all shared JS, `sw.js`, the four tools and `faq_data.py` parse. Browser pass over all eight via same-origin iframes at **1200px and 375px**: every expected new string present, **0 console errors, 0 leading-slash links, 0 horizontal scroll at either width**, the mini-TOC picking up all four new h2s, the corrected APA sentence rendering as R² = .25 / β = .50, all 13 new link targets returning 200, all five `datasets.html` anchors present, and dark mode verified (bg #0b1120, `--link` #6f7bf2). **One check worth repeating on every future run: after rebuilding the index, search it for the terms the run just added.** Doing that here caught two of the seven as **unsearchable** — §1.3 wrote only "Cohen's κ" so a student typing *kappa* got nothing (search v2's `squash`/`flexRe` normalise separators, not Greek), and §2.8 explained the phenomenon without ever naming **attenuation**.   - **25 Jul 2026 — eleventh run (the regression core, the shape of a dataset, and three statistics the site reported but never taught).** The staleness ranking (last commit that substantively changed this file, mass passes excluded) cleared to a 3 Jul tier of three and a 6 Jul tier: `stats-3/multiple-regression` (§3.1), `stats-3/ancova` (§3.4), `stats-4/survival-analysis` (§4.8), `data/tidy-data` (§2.1, 10:15) and `data/wide-vs-long-data` (§2.7, tie-broken toward the shape thread) — **the first run to reach Stats 3's regression spine**. **Three absences, each confirmed by a sitewide grep rather than assumed, and all three the same defect: a statistic the site reports and never defines.** (1) **Standardized coefficients.** β appears in `problems.html` (Problem 23, whose whole point is that raw coefficients aren't comparable — and which §3.1 itself links), is typeset in `writing/reporting-statistics-apa`, gets a leading-zero rule in a FAQ, and is printed three times in §3.1's own injected APA sentence (β = .38 / .21 / −.07) — while **no lesson said what it is**. §3.1 gained a section; β = *b* × SD_x ÷ SD_y was checked against a z-scored refit (identical to 6 dp), β = *r* in simple regression exactly, and Problem 23's own .419 / .310 and adj *R*² = .3287 reproduced. (2) **ANCOVA had no effect size at all** — not "η²", not the phrase "effect size" — on a lesson whose SPSS steps say to tick *Estimates of effect size* and whose APA sentence reports ηp² = .11. §3.4 gained the section: ηp² = *F*·df₁ ÷ (*F*·df₁ + df₂) verified against the sums-of-squares route exactly (0.109911 both ways), and the box's *F*(1, 57) = 6.84 → .1071. **The "partial η² comes out larger" claim was simulated, not assumed** — 2,000 randomized datasets give mean .089 against an unadjusted .049, ANCOVA larger in **79.6%** of runs, while a confounded example runs the other way (.36 → .11), so the sentence states both directions instead of the convenient one. (3) **Proportional hazards was named, assumed and never checked.** `Schoenfeld` returned **one hit sitewide, in `plan.html`**, which tells readers to check PH with residuals no lesson explains — run 9's Durbin-Watson finding exactly. §4.8 gained three checks in rising formality plus four repairs; the log-minus-log vertical gap = log HR was verified algebraically and numerically (7 dp at five times), and `cox.zph` run live in **survival 3.8.3** (*p* = .79 when PH holds, 2.8e-16 when the hazards cross). **Also:** §4.8's interactive already prints "> 36 mo" when the censoring slider hides the median, and the prose never named that state — it now teaches **not reached** and the landmark-rate alternative. §2.1 carried a dangling forward reference ("you'll meet `pivot_longer`/`pivot_wider` later in this course") **with no link**, now pointed at §2.7, plus `reproducible-workflows` for its raw-file-read-only paragraph. §2.7 gained "the shape decides who gets dropped" (wide has to be a rectangle, so RM-ANOVA deletes the whole participant a mixed model would keep) and **SPSS's Data → Restructure path**, which the lesson demanded twice and never gave. **FAQ freshness:** five swaps, each displacing an answer that restated its own lesson (§2.1's also duplicated §2.7's FAQ #1 across pages), all duplicate-checked against the full 291 first — a predictor-normality candidate was killed because `assumptions-of-regression` owns it verbatim. What shipped: "My R² is low but my predictors are significant. Is the model useless?" (*F*(1, 998) = 10.08, *p* = .002 at R² = .01, computed), "Should I analyze change scores (post minus pre) instead of using ANCOVA?" (**the SE ratio √((1 + r)/2)** confirmed by 4,000-replication simulation at five values of ρ, within .015 of theory at every one), "How many participants does a survival study need?" (Schoenfeld's 4(z + z)² ÷ (ln HR)² gives 65.3 events at HR = 2, but an independent log-rank simulation gives **77% power at 66 events and 81% at 70**, so the answer says "roughly 65 to 70" rather than parroting the formula), "My table has one row per person and several measurement columns. Is that untidy?", "My wide sheet has no ID column. Can I still pivot it to long?". FAQ em-dashes **10 → 8**. **software.js:** the batch's three entries re-verified against SPSS 31/32 + JASP menu reality, **no step changed**; four tips added where the new prose created an obligation (SPSS's *Standardized Coefficients* column; the *Partial Eta Squared* label and why it can't share a table with unadjusted η²; the two SPSS PH-check routes; "not reached"). Still **34 entries**. **Snippets — one snippet made a claim that is false for most readers.** §2.7's R half asserted `identical(wide, wide2)` with the comment "TRUE -> the reshape is lossless". Run live on **tidyr 1.3.2**: TRUE for a tibble, **FALSE for a plain `data.frame`**, which is exactly what `read.csv()` hands a student — so the one reader who actually checks concludes the pivot ate their data. Now `all.equal(as.data.frame(…))`, verified TRUE both ways, with the reason in the comment. Same capybara, different table." **Backlog item — the one I picked was already done, so I built the checker instead.** P38 run 2's deferred "`regression-diagnostics` re-randomises its cloud on every scenario click" was cleared by a later P39 commit; verified in-browser across eight scenario switches (good = 0.81, funnel = 0.51, curve = 0.01 returning **identically** every revisit, and "New sample" moving it to 0.86), so **P38's tracker line is stale, not the site**.   - **23 Jul 2026 — tenth run (the never-refreshed toolkit foundations, and the practice-problem map closed).** The staleness ranking (last commit that substantively changed this file, mass passes excluded) cleared to five lessons no run had touched as its subject, forming a coherent *measured variable → clean data* thread: `methods/variables-and-operationalization` (§1.2, 4 Jul), `methods/bias-and-blinding` (§1.10, 4 Jul), `methods/preregistration-and-open-science` (§1.12, 4 Jul), `data/codebooks-and-documentation` (§2.2, 6 Jul) and `data/data-entry-and-validation` (§2.3, 6 Jul) — the **first run to reach the Data course** as a subject rather than a cross-link target. **The gaps were the thread's own spine.** §1.2's callout said "randomization and controls exist precisely to close off rival stories" and linked neither, and its "your operational definitions *are* your data" pointed nowhere near where they get written down — now → `experimental-design-and-randomization` and → `codebooks-and-documentation`. §1.10 named "selection and attrition bias" and "better sampling" with no links; the attrition bullet now hands off to `stats-4/missing-data` (non-random dropout is the design-stage face of MNAR) and "sampling" to `sampling-methods`. §1.12's sample-size decision now reaches `effect-size-and-power` (a prereg's *n* has to be power-justified) and its "disguising exploration as confirmation" reaches `ethics/questionable-research-practices`. §2.2 closes the loop back to `variables-and-operationalization` (the codebook is where an operational definition is written down for good); §2.3's "influential outlier" clause, which jumped straight to `stats-2/regression-diagnostics`, now first hands the error-vs-genuine-extreme distinction to its sibling `outliers-in-practice`. **FAQ freshness:** five swaps, each displacing an answer that restated its lesson's own h2, duplicate-checked against all 291 existing Q&As first — **two candidates were killed before writing** (a "how do I choose between measures" answer §1.2's own FAQ #3 already owns, and a composite/reverse-scoring answer §2.6 `transformations-and-recoding` owns). (the reductionism worry, uncovered anywhere on the site), "How do I blind a study when I can't hide the treatment from participants?" (assessor-blinding, objective endpoints, PROBE = Prospective Randomized Open Blinded Endpoint — the lesson taught only single/double-blind), "Can I preregister a study that analyzes data I already have?" (the manual-entry lesson modernized: straightlining, unit confusion, silent encoding changes, duplicate submissions). Every answer is conceptual — no numeric claim to verify this run, unlike the simulation-heavy prior batches — so PROBE's expansion and the secondary-data registry practice were the facts checked. **software.js — the sweep is complete.** My five lessons carry no `SOFTWARE` entry (correct: conceptual/toolkit).
